<meta>This is the title page of Michael Maier's "Silentium Post Clamores," published in 1617. It serves as a formal defense of the Rosicrucian Fraternity during a period of intense public speculation.</meta>

# SILENCE
## AFTER THE
# OUTCRIES,
### THAT IS, AN
# APOLOGETIC TREATISE, IN WHICH
the causes are presented and demonstrated, not only for the outcries <note>original: "clamorum," referring here to the public manifestos or "shouts" that announced the order's existence</note> or revelations of the German **FRATERNITY** of the Rose Cross <note>original: "R. C." or Rosicrucians, a mysterious alchemical and spiritual brotherhood</note>, but also for their Silence—that is, the lack of response given to the petitions of individuals. This work is written together with a refutation of those who bear them ill-will.

By the Author **MICHAEL MAIER** *Count of the Imperial Consistory <term>Consistory: an administrative council of the Holy Roman Empire</term>, Knight <term>Knight Exempt: a title often granted to court physicians or officials, implying specific legal privileges</term>, and Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine.*

<image-desc>An oval engraving set within a decorative frame. On the left side, a group of approximately seven men, dressed in traditional early 17th-century academic or fraternal attire (gowns and hats), are gathered in conversation or observation. They face a nude male statue standing on a stone pedestal on the right. The statue holds a finger to its lips, the classical gesture for silence (associated with Harpocrates). A sphinx, symbol of mystery and riddles, sits at the foot of the pedestal. The background depicts an architectural space with a stone archway and a doorway.</image-desc>

->Frankfurt. Published by **LUCAS JENNIS.**<-

->1617<-

<summary>This title page introduces Michael Maier's defense of the Rosicrucian Order, explaining why the group remained silent despite the public fervor and numerous petitions following their initial manifestos. It identifies Maier as a high-ranking imperial official and physician, lending authority to this alchemical and philosophical "apology."</summary>

<meta>This page follows the title page of "Silentium Post Clamores" (1617). It contains a prefatory poem by Michael Maier, using classical and mythological allegories to justify why the Rosicrucian Order has remained silent despite the public's demands for information.</meta>

# EPIGRAM BY THE AU-
## THOR.

The most celebrated band of Indian Brahmans <term>Brachmana: Sages or priests of ancient India, whom 17th-century scholars like Maier often regarded as the original possessors of secret, divine wisdom</term>
Drew learning to themselves by their own love;
(Many of their number were received by right of their fellowship
After they had been taught by the wondrous art<unclear>)</unclear>:
Swearing a sacred silence from the vial of the covenant,
Which Tantalus himself carried in his right hand, he drinks.
For in such a way did so great a matter demand that secrets be covered,
Lest a talkative tongue betray the silent faith.
For indeed, it is said that Tantalus <note>original: "Tantalus," a figure in Greek mythology punished for stealing the gods' nectar and revealing their secrets to mortals</note> scattered among the common people
The deeds of the Gods that should have been kept silent in secret ways:
Because of this, he is pressed by thirst though surrounded by waves,
And his apples are unable to relieve his hunger.
From this example, the Order of German blood <note>A reference to the Rosicrucian Fraternity, founded in Germany</note> rightly takes care for itself,
Lest it give its sacred gifts—or its roses—to dogs.
When teachings <term>Dogmata: The core principles or doctrines of the Order</term> have proceeded from a faithful mind,
Which men of little faith did not wish to believe;
The Order holds a safe silence hereafter (just as before), lest anyone
Proclaim this a "Phantom" <term>Phantasma: A common criticism of the time was that the Rosicrucians did not actually exist and were merely a "phantom" or a literary hoax</term> or a series of fictitious deceits:
Let the evil mind of the ungrateful receive its worthy rewards,
And let there be glory for each according to his own merits.

<summary>A cautionary poem using the myth of Tantalus to explain why the Rosicrucian Order (the "Order of German blood") maintains strict secrecy to protect its sacred knowledge from the "ungrateful" public.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucians, Tantalus, secrecy, Brahmans, silence, German Fraternity, allegory, occult philosophy</keywords>

# PREFACE
## *to the reader.*

<image-desc>An ornate rectangular woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical scrollwork and stylized foliage. At the center is a decorative vase or chalice. The scrollwork on either side incorporates the forms of mythical birds or griffins facing outward.</image-desc>

A certain Talos <term>Talos: In Greek mythology, a giant bronze automaton or man of bronze, often said to have been forged by Hephaestus to protect the island of Crete</term>, appointed over Crete by Jove <note>original: "Ioue," referring to Jupiter/Zeus</note> with bronze feet, is imagined to have excelled with such speed that he could go around Crete three times daily and carry the tablets of the laws through the towns (which are said to have been a hundred in number). To him, honest reader, that Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross <note>original: "Fraternitas R. C.," the Rosicrucians</note>, which is now on everyone’s lips, seems to many to be not dissimilar. For they not only think it "plays the Cretan" <term>Cretizare: A Latin idiom meaning "to lie" or "to act like a Cretan," based on the ancient proverb that "all Cretans are liars"</term>, but also that it carries tablets, not of laws, but full of trifles, throughout Europe to de- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword "ferre" indicates the word is "deferre," meaning to carry down, report, or distribute</note>

<meta>catchword: carry / report</meta>

<summary>Maier begins his preface by comparing the Rosicrucian Brotherhood to the mythological bronze giant Talos, noting that critics accuse the Order of spreading lies and trifles across Europe rather than divine laws.</summary>
<keywords>Talos, Crete, Rosicrucian Brotherhood, mythology, rumors, Cretan lie, Michael Maier</keywords>

Maier is addressing the intense public criticism and slander directed at the Rosicrucian Brotherhood.</meta>

distribute [them]. From this, so many 
**slanders** and **viperous tongues**

are held and proclaimed to be deceptive 
**Ixions** <term>Ixion: In Greek mythology, Ixion attempted to seduce Juno (Hera) but was tricked by Jupiter into consorting with a cloud-clone of the goddess, becoming a symbol of one who pursues illusions.</term> or creators of monsters, 
since they have joined with a **cloud** of insane **opinion** 
instead of **Juno**, the goddess of Riches; 
unhappy parents, like **Autolycus** <term>Autolycus: A legendary thief and trickster of Greek myth, the son of Hermes, known for his ability to make his thefts invisible or unrecognizable.</term>, 
who infested the places near **Parnassus** 
with his thefts. But those [critics], in 
my opinion, employ a judgment too

that is contrary to the truth, and 
nothing is asserted that is adverse to 
good morals: **equity**, **reason**, and **experience** 
themselves support them.

<summary>Michael Maier defends the Rosicrucian Brotherhood against accusations of fraud and trickery, arguing that critics are overzealous and pedantic in their condemnation. He uses a series of classical metaphors to characterize the slanderers as misguided and the Order as fundamentally moral.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of Michael Maier’s "Silentium Post Clamores" (1617). Maier is arguing that the internal consistency and ancient roots of the Rosicrucian manifestos prove their truthfulness.</meta>

of the most ancient authors; the order and harmony of matters and all circumstances are known to serve the cause of truth, rather than falsehood. Nor indeed is it possible that a **deception** <note>original: "fucus," literally a red dye or rouge used as a disguise or cosmetic</note> be found more lifelike than a natural color, or **vice** more becoming than **virtue**, or that common **rumors** be found more truthful than the evidence of one's own eyes. 

If that were to happen, nothing at all could be trusted—neither **reason** nor the **healthy senses**—but instead, everything would have to be called into doubt, as the **Pyrrhonists** and the **New Academics** <term>Pyrrhonists and New Academics: Two schools of ancient Greek philosophy that advocated for extreme skepticism, holding that certain knowledge is impossible.</term> believe. 

In my view, those who published those two writings, the **Fama** and the **Confessio** <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the founding manifestos of the Rosicrucian movement.</note>, seem to have fulfilled their purpose; they are worthy of no **censor’s rod** <note>original: "censoria virgula," a mark used by ancient critics to denote a passage to be deleted or corrected</note> (a mark they do not even recognize, but send back to its authors). But because human customs and this present age are such that they ma-

-> * 3 <-

<summary>Maier argues that the internal consistency and ancient wisdom found in the Rosicrucian manifestos prove their authenticity, asserting that if such clear evidence were false, all human reason and sensory perception would be invalid.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues Michael Maier’s defense of the Rosicrucians. The previous page ended with the syllable "ma-", which completes here as "maledicos" (slanderers). Maier is explaining why the Rosicrucians remain silent in the face of criticism, comparing their silence to the professional restraint of a doctor.</meta>

**slanderers** <note>original: "maledicos," referring to those who speak ill of the Rosicrucian Order</note> back to peace and a sounder mind through their own **silence** (just as **physicians** <note>original: "Medici"</note> treat the **delirious** <term>frenzied or "phrenetic" patients: in 17th-century medicine, those suffering from an inflammation of the mind or brain</term>), rather than provoking them into an increase of **bile-driven passion** <note>In the medical theory of the time, an excess of "yellow bile" was thought to cause anger and irritability.</note> through lengthy replies—replies which they could certainly offer with truth on their side. 

In the meantime, although they do not need our defense or assistance, and although I expect nothing from them except that **goodwill** which good men naturally extend to the good, I could not help but cast my **vote**—and **not a black one**—for the truth. <term>calculum non nigellum: literally "not a little black pebble." In ancient Greek trials, a white pebble signaled acquittal and a black pebble signaled a vote for condemnation.</term> 

I have done this lest the Truth appear oppressed by the spite of those who do not even know how to write the letter **Theta**, <term>Theta: The first letter of the Greek word "Thanatos" (Death). It was used by judges as a shorthand mark on ballots to signify a sentence of death.</term> rather than exonerated by the sincerity of those who judge correctly. For there are many similar to that **laborer** <note>original: "cerdoni," a term for a low-status craftsman or cobbler</note> who, being **illiterate**, voted to banish **Aristides** the Just... <note>Maier refers to a famous story from ancient Athens: an illiterate citizen, not recognizing the statesman Aristides, asked Aristides himself to write the name "Aristides" on a shard of pottery used for voting for exile, simply because he was tired of hearing everyone call him "The Just."</note>

<summary>Maier explains that the Rosicrucians remain silent to avoid further provoking their "delirious" critics, much like doctors treating a patient. He justifies his own writing as a "white pebble" vote in favor of the Order's truth against the ignorant and envious.</summary>

<keywords>silence, physicians, bile, acquittal, Theta, Aristides the Just, defense of the Rosicrucians</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page completes the story of Aristides the Just, which began on the previous page. The text concludes Michael Maier’s preface to the reader, contrasting the unlearned critics of the Rosicrucians with the fair-minded scholar.</meta>

...who had **deserved excellently of the Republic** <note>original: "optimè de Repub. meritum." Aristides was a famous Athenian statesman known for his integrity.</note>, though not even **recognized by sight**; the laborer condemned him along with the others for this reason alone: because he was **too just**. 

There is no doubt that there are many similar people in this current estimation. But as we know these people are intended for **hoes and plows** <note>original: "ligones & aratra." Maier is making a sharp distinction between the working class, whom he deems unfit to judge his work, and the intellectual elite.</note>, and not for **scholarship or the halls of justice** <note>original: "literas & tribunalia"</note>, we thus dismiss them. And you, **Kind Reader** <term>Candide lector: A standard Latin greeting meaning a "bright," "pure," or "unbiased" reader.</term>, being exempt from their number, we

->commend to God.<-

->Farewell.<-

# INDEX OF THE INDIVIDUAL
## CHAPTERS of this Treatise.

### CAP. I.

*That the hiding places of Nature <note>original: "Naturae latebras." Maier suggests that nature still holds many undiscovered mysteries.</note> have not been so exhausted that all her secrets are known; rather, more remains hidden than is known—specifically those things which are true and which, as effects, bear witness to their own cause.*

### CAP. II.

*That the secrets of Nature are primarily of the sort that the German Brotherhood <note>A reference to the Rosicrucian Order (Fraternitas R.C.), a mysterious group of scholars and alchemists whose manifestos had recently appeared in Germany.</note> has confessed.*

### CAP. III.

*That among the secrets of the German Brotherhood of the R.C. <term>R.C.: Abbreviation for Rose Cross (Rosae Crucis).</term> is the Universal Medicine <term>Universal Medicine: Also known as the Panacea, a legendary alchemical substance capable of curing all diseases and prolonging life.</term>, the primary Gift of God, second only to the knowledge of Him.*

### CAP. IV.

*That such a Medicine exists hidden in Nature is clear from demonstrations.*

<meta>catchword: Fuiſſe (That there was...)</meta>

### CHAPTER V.

*It is well known that in ancient times, among other nations, there were Philosophical Fraternities or Colleges that uniquely cherished the secrets of nature and this Medicine <term>Medicine: In this context, Maier refers to the "Universal Medicine" or Philosopher’s Stone, believed by alchemists to cure all illness and transform matter.</term>, and passed them down, as if by hand, through many centuries to successors chosen from the entire company of Philosophers.*

### CHAPTER VI.

*From this, it should not seem absurd that even now some society exists, especially since it has revealed itself through its writings and deeds.*

### CHAPTER VII.

*If, therefore, the German Brotherhood <note>Maier is referring to the Rosicrucian Order (Fraternitas R.C.), which had recently become a sensation in Europe.</note> has endured for many years up to the present—as it itself testifies—it aimed at the honor of God and the benefit of mankind, should it ever be made manifest, just as has occurred in our own time.*

### CHAPTER VIII.

*If the German society was to be made known, it could not or should not have been revealed in any other way than was done through those two writings, the* Fama *<note>original: "Famae." Referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614), the first Rosicrucian manifesto.</note> and the* Confession *<note>original: "Confessionis." Referring to the *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the second manifesto.</note>.*

# CHAPTER IX.

*That nothing is contained in those books which is contrary to Reason, Nature, Experience, or the possibility of the matter.*

# CHAPTER X.

*That it is rightly said by the Brotherhood that "many are called, but very few are chosen," <note>A biblical allusion to Matthew 22:14, used here to explain the selective nature of the Rosicrucian initiation.</note> after considering the reasons.*

# CHAPTER XI.

*The Egyptian deities, Sigalion <term>Sigalion: A deity of silence in late antiquity, often synonymous with Harpocrates.</term> or Harpocrates <term>Harpocrates: The Greek interpretation of the Egyptian god Horus the Child, typically depicted with a finger to his lips, symbolizing silence and secrecy.</term>, were placed at altars alongside Sphinxes to denote silence—a practice that was also observed as a five-year rule among the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras were famously required to observe a strict five-year period of silence during their education.</note>. This same silence is observed by the said Brotherhood of the R.C. <note>The Rosicrucians.</note>, lest the secrets of Nature <term>arcana Naturæ: The hidden mysteries or "deep secrets" of the natural world, often referring to alchemical or spiritual truths.</term> be cheapened by the common crowd.*

# CHAPTER XII.

*Since they indeed had reason to reveal themselves to the world to some extent and to test the judgments of the more learned, a reason was later given to them why they should shroud themselves in silence for a time, and overcome and endure the first "paroxysm" <note>original: "paroxysmum." Maier uses this medical term to describe the sudden "fever" or outburst of public controversy and wild speculation that followed the publication of the Rosicrucian manifestos.</note> with patience.*

## Chapter XIII.

*For the public revelation of these men has been received in many ways through rumor and declaration: while some proclaim this entire business to be a worldly illusion <note>original: "Phanthasmate."</note> and a monstrous delirium. Those who judge in this way are either unrefined and ignorant of all things, or they oppose Alchemy out of envy or lack of skill, or out of an excessive self-love <term>philautia: A Greek-derived term for vanity or narcissism, often used by Renaissance authors to describe intellectual arrogance.</term> they ascribe all possible knowledge to themselves. Here, a response is given to the first group, namely the ignorant.*

## Chapter XIV.

*A response directed specifically to the adversaries of Alchemy <term>Chymiæ: At this time, chemistry and alchemy were not yet distinct disciplines; Maier uses the term to refer to the "Hermetic Art" of transforming matter.</term> under this name.*

## Chapter XV.

*A response to those who are haughty in their learning and swollen with self-love.*

## Chapter XVI.

*Others attribute [the matter] to Necromancy <note>original: "Nigromantiæ." Referring to black magic or the summoning of spirits, a common accusation against secret societies.</note> and every kind of superstition; a response is given to them.*

## Chapter XVII.

*Very many people, who deny neither the truth of Alchemy nor the integrity of the Brotherhood, yet realize they are not deemed worthy of such a great fellowship <term>sodalitio: A formal society or association; here referring to the Rosicrucian Order.</term>, [their] mind,* <meta>catchword: animum,</meta>

### CHAPTER XVIII.

When these people are finally discovered to be deceivers, thieves, or robbers, they are afflicted with the punishments they deserve.*

# SILENCE AFTER THE
## CLAMOR,

->THAT IS,<-

### AN APOLOGETIC
### TREATISE <note>original: "Tractatus Apologetici." In this context, an "apology" is not an expression of regret, but a formal defense of a cause or belief—in this case, a defense of the Rosicrucian Order.</note>

### CHAPTER I.

> *The hiding places of Nature have not been so*
> *exhausted that all her secrets*
> *have become known; instead, more remains hidden*
> *than is known—namely, those things which*
> *are true, and which, as effects, bear witness*
> *to their cause.*

Nature, which reigns diffused through the four
spheres of the Elements <term>Elements: In the early modern period, these were the four fundamental substances—Earth, Water, Air, and Fire—which were believed to compose all physical matter.</term> and the heavens—
that is, the universe—has produced as many hidden 
mysteries of its own, by the will of the Supreme 
Creator <note>original: "Creatoatoris." This is a typographical error in the original Latin text for "Creatoris."</note>, as there are species of things...

<meta>catchword: rio.</meta> <note>The catchword "rio" completes the word "mysterio" or "mysteriorum," meaning "mysteries."</note>

<summary>This page begins the first chapter of the treatise, arguing that the natural world is far more mysterious and complex than humans currently understand. Michael Maier establishes that the Creator has hidden truths within nature that are yet to be revealed.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, elements, universe, Creator, hidden secrets, natural philosophy, mysteries, apologetic defense</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening argument of Chapter I. The previous page ended with the observation that nature contains as many hidden mysteries as there are species of things. This page begins by completing the word "mysteries" (mysteriorum) from the previous page's catchword.</meta>

...of mysteries, she recognizes hiding places, in which she has hidden whatever she wished to be removed from the common eye or untouched by hand. She does this not as the greedy and envious are accustomed to do, driven by the sting of vice, but so that she might apply a spur toward virtue <note>original: "virtutem." In this context, virtue refers to both moral character and the active pursuit of excellence and knowledge.</note> in those who are slow and lazy. This spur encourages them to seek out what is concealed, to value what is found, and to protect what is precious.

For just as those things which lie open before one's feet are often unappreciated, unless seasoned by rarity, so those things which lie in hiding are usually welcomed—unless they become commonplace through frequent use. This is not always due to the inherent essence <term>Essence: The fundamental nature or quality of a thing that makes it what it is, regardless of its availability.</term> of each thing, but also due to the inconstancy of human minds and the hunger for novelty.

Very many things lie hidden in the center of the earth, or are buried in the caverns of mountains, on the shores and at the bottom of the Ocean, the sea, or the rivers. These are valued at a great price because they are rare to find; but if they were as plentiful as sand, they would be reckoned as having the same value as sand. 

But it is the task of the Philosopher <note>In the 17th century, a "Philosopher" was a seeker of wisdom who studied "Natural Philosophy," the precursor to modern science.</note> not only to investigate those things which the common people desire, but also—and indeed more so—those things hidden in other species of nature, whether these things appear common to the eye or not. Who has ever sufficiently [investigated] the nature of man—that is, the gifts and goodness of the soul as well as the body...

<meta>catchword: inda-</meta> <note>The catchword "inda-" likely leads to the word "indagavit," meaning "has investigated" or "has tracked down."</note>

<summary>Maier argues that Nature hides her secrets to motivate seekers and maintain the value of discovery, noting that rarity often dictates perceived worth. He defines the true Philosopher's role as look beyond common desires to investigate the hidden depths of all natural things, including human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Nature's secrets, virtue, rarity and value, philosophy, human nature, hidden knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the mysteries of nature, specifically moving from external nature to the internal "small world" of the human body and soul. It connects to the previous page's mention of investigating "other species."</meta>

...investigated <note>Completing the thought from the previous page regarding the investigation of hidden things.</note>, in which anatomists and physicians, occupied for so many centuries, always bring forth new discoveries of that small World <note>original: "parui illius Mundi." This refers to the "Microcosm," a traditional philosophical concept where the human body is seen as a miniature reflection of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note> to such an extent that it has hardly been possible to describe the body sufficiently even up to this point? To say nothing of the movements of the mind <note>original: "motibus animi," referring to the emotions or passions.</note> and the rational soul itself; are not most of these things thought to lie hidden in a dark cave, as it were, and a gloomy prison, even though they are present within us? 

For Metaphysical matters <term>Metaphysical: Referred to in the Latin text as "Hyperphysica," these are subjects that transcend the physical world, such as the essence of the soul or spiritual causes.</term> which pertain to this subject are veiled in such a cloud of ignorance that hardly anything of their causes and reasons has become known to Philosophers. The remaining works of nature are of such variety that they are beyond counting, and of such difficulty that they do not reveal themselves through investigation. 

When that most wise Socrates <note>Socrates (c. 470–399 BCE) was a foundational figure in Western philosophy.</note>—considered such even by the judgment of the Oracle <note>The Oracle of Delphi, which famously declared that no one was wiser than Socrates.</note>—observed this, he used to profess that he knew nothing, except for this one thing: that he knew nothing. For just as the size of this earth which we touch is vast in relation to the human body, but almost nothing in proportion to the heavens—so the knowledge of the most learned man, if compared with the whole of nature, is almost nothing; yet, if compared with the unlearned masses, it is of no small importance. From this we can easily conjecture that far more things lie hidden in the concealed treasures of nature...

<summary>This page argues that despite centuries of medical study, the human body and soul remain largely mysterious, comparing the limits of human knowledge to the vastness of nature and the heavens.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the limitations of human knowledge, specifically transitioning from the skepticism of Socrates mentioned on the previous page to a discussion on the relationship between Nature and the human "Arts" (science and technology). It completes the sentence "knew nothin[g]" from page 15.</meta>

...lie hidden, rather than being truly known by men. Nor indeed should anyone think that all hidden things are to be revealed, or that such progress can be made in the arts and sciences that nothing more can be discovered—for discovery is the gift of nature. Rather, let him think that the round circles of Nature are so interconnected that one holds the other like a chain, which, as long as the world exists, shall never be ended.

Natural things are the **Bodies** <term>Bodies: In this context, 'Corpora' refers to physical matter or material objects.</term> which are crowded together in this world, beyond which nothing especially worthy of consideration occurs, unless they be entirely spiritual and divine. **Art** <term>Art: From the Latin 'Ars', referring to human skill, craft, or technology used to manipulate the natural world.</term>, as a subject of nature, makes use of her; thus it observes her footsteps and imitates her in many things; indeed, it sometimes corrects her when she wanders and reminds her when she has forgotten her duty, creating or introducing nothing truly new into the world. Although, by a pleasing manipulation <note>original: "mangonio." This term refers to the art of "doctoring" or cleverly arranging things, often used in a trade or alchemical context to describe how humans combine natural ingredients.</note> of two or more natural things, it not infrequently produces a third thing—whether it should be called natural or artificial, I know not. 

From the observation of natural things, **Art** was born, with **Method** as the midwife and **Reason** as the mother. Therefore, if the aim of art does not correspond to nature, it seems to be born of a spurious concept and is not genuine; that is, it is necessary that the **Theorems** <term>Theorems: 'Theoremata' here refers to the foundational principles or rules of a science.</term> of the arts and sciences [correspond] to the works of nature...

<summary>This page explores the interconnectedness of nature and the role of human "Art" (science) as an imitator and corrector of natural processes, rather than a creator of entirely new things. It famously describes Art as being born from Reason and Method.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Art vs Science, Bodies, Reason, Method, Chain of Nature, human knowledge, manipulation of nature</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the relationship between human Art (science/technology) and Nature. It completes the thought from page 16, which ended with the word "natu...", suggesting "nature, it is necessary for it..." to agree.</meta>

...to agree, and in no way to differ from them:

For example, those celestial lights, the **Sun** and the **Moon**, are governed by nature and moved by regular motions in such a way that if their centers ever come into a straight line with the center of the earth, an eclipse of either the Sun or the Moon occurs, according to the alignment or opposition of the lights; **Art** <term>Art: Here, 'Ars' refers to the human science of astronomy or the skill of calculation.</term> investigates their motions, and if it has discovered the true ones, it will also be able to know the true time of their darkening. Hence, since all eclipses are predicted, it is evident that the celestial motions have been investigated by **Art** in a way that corresponds with **Nature**.

Similarly, if **Art** has investigated the true matter and form of gold and silver, together with the efficient cause, and knows how to join those causes (as the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophi." In this period, this term usually refers to natural philosophers or alchemists who studied the transformation of matter.</note> testify), it will also produce a true natural work. When this product is shown to many craftsmen, it is most certain that the theoretical study of **Art** walks in step and agrees with the work of **Nature**.

In the same way, would that it were so with **medicine** and the other arts (for regarding the pure sciences no doubt remains), then principles would always agree with their goal, and effects with their cause, and testi- <meta>catchword: monio (testimony)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that human science (Art) is validated when it can perfectly predict or replicate natural phenomena, such as eclipses or the formation of precious metals.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, eclipses, alchemy, gold and silver, medicine, natural philosophy, Art vs Nature</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence from page 17, concluding the argument that the principles of science must be validated by observable results.</meta>

...mony, which is observed in many instances to happen very rarely nowadays. This, then, is the true investigation of nature through **Art** <term>Art: Here meaning "Ars," the human application of skill, science, or technology.</term>, to which the work of nature itself bears witness.

Nor should we grieve with **Plato**, who was devoted solely to **Theory** <term>Theory: From the Greek *theoria*, meaning abstract contemplation of universal truths, separate from physical matter.</term>, that **Geometry** was adapted from pure thought and incorporeal things to those which are perceived by the senses by **Eudoxus of Cnidus** and his teacher **Archytas of Tarentum**. Of these two, Archytas gave a famous proof of his skill by creating a mechanical flying dove <note>Archytas (4th century BCE) was a Greek mathematician and engineer famous for this early "robot," a wooden bird allegedly powered by a steam jet.</note>, while Eudoxus was the first to demonstrate the principles and difficult proofs of **Geometry** through sensory perception and the use of physical instruments.

Rather, we should acknowledge with a grateful mind that the fruit has appeared after the flower, and that the daughter has been born of the mother—that is, that **practice** has been witnessed coming forth from **Theory**. If it were otherwise, the arts would not be arts at all, but would be no different from **Sophistries**, which **Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a philosopher and mystic who sought a universal system of knowledge.</note> calls "Non-beings" <note>original: "non Entia." Things that exist only in the mind and have no reality in the physical or divine world.</note>. He asserts that the minds and memories of men are so heavily weighed down by these—being merely imaginary or at least artificial constructs—that they leave scarcely any room for perceiving things that are true and real.

# CHAPTER TWO.

## *The secrets of nature are especially of the kind that the German Brotherhood <note>This refers to the Rosicrucian Order, a mysterious collective of scholars and alchemists whose manifestos, published in Germany in the early 17th century, called for a universal reformation of science and religion.</note> has declared.*

There are, to be sure, powers or properties in natural things that do not strike the eyes or the senses unless they are manifested by **Experience** <term>Experience: Here meaning "Experientia," the act of empirical observation and testing in the physical world.</term>, just as it appears most clearly in the magnet. Its twin virtue—of turning itself toward the pole of the world (or a place closest to it) and of attracting iron to itself—would have been known to no man had it not shown itself by the very fact of its action. For **Reason** <term>Reason: Translated from "Ratio," referring to the human capacity for logic and abstract thought, which the author argues is insufficient on its own to discover the hidden laws of nature.</term> alone, even that of the most discerning person, could not foresee or point it out beforehand, although **Reason** knows very well how to judge the discovery once it has been made. Therefore, the arts discovered through experience are found to have the most certain effects, provided they are perfected with the assistance of **Reason**. If they proceed from **Reason** alone, or from the imagination, they are for the most part—though beautiful in appearance—nevertheless in fruit... <meta>The sentence continues on the following page with the catchword "ina-", likely completing the word "inanes," meaning "void" or "empty."</meta>

<summary>The author argues that nature possesses hidden powers, like magnetism, which can only be discovered through empirical experience rather than pure logic. This serves to validate the claims of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood regarding the existence of secret natural knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>magnetism, experience vs. reason, Rosicrucians, German Brotherhood, secrets of nature, empirical observation</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous one, completing the sentence regarding arts that proceed from Reason alone without the validation of Experience.</meta>

...are believed to be empty. For since there is such a great variety of **human speculation** <term>human speculation: Translated from "humanæ speculationis," referring to theoretical or abstract thinking not grounded in physical reality.</term>, it very rarely receives the vote of approval from that **Experience** <note>original: "Experientia"</note>, which is the cause of the futility of many arts introduced by human ingenuity. For example, let the art of **haruspicy** <note>The ancient practice of divining the future by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals.</note> and the art of **augury** <note>The practice of interpreting the will of the gods by observing the flight or behavior of birds.</note> serve as illustrations, as well as those things which depend solely on the shifts of fortune. 

However, even though true **axioms** <term>axioms: From "axiomata," these are the self-evident principles or established laws upon which a science or art is built.</term> of the arts were discovered at the beginning, and were equipped with precepts or rules leading to a certain goal, it nevertheless happens not infrequently that with the **passage of time** <note>original: "successu temporum"</note> and the variety of intellects, they fall into abuse or a corrupted sense. They may be twisted into a form that is very far removed from nature and never agrees with the properties lying hidden in its bosom. Hence, arts of this kind hardly ever reach their intended goal, except perhaps by a stroke of luck. 

For just as **gold** <note>original: "aurum"</note>, which is by its own nature **homogeneous** <term>homogeneous: Of the same kind or nature throughout; pure.</term> and perfectly pure, acquires **heterogeneous dross** <term>heterogeneous dross: Translated from "heterogeneas fæces," referring to external impurities or "filth" that contaminate a pure substance.</term> through constant use—by which it is darkened and hindered from its natural splendor—so also the **sciences** <note>original: "scientiæ"</note> are in themselves indeed pure and adapted to their own axioms, and they correspond to their goal; but the excess of the minds of those reading or employing them—that is, for...

<summary>This page argues that abstract human speculation often leads to futile "arts" like divination, and warns that even true sciences can be corrupted over time. by the capacity of those interpreting them, they are contaminated and decline from nobility to pretenses, or from truth to **Sophistry** <term>Sophistry: Derived from "Sophisticen," the practice of using clever but fallacious and deceptive arguments.</term>. To meet this evil and resist it through the passing centuries, **God, the Best and Greatest** <note>original: "Deus Opt. Max." (Deus Optimus Maximus), a title signifying God's supreme goodness and power.</note>, out of His admirable kindness, raises up restorers and critics of the arts. Like **assayers** <term>assayers: From "docimastæ," specialists who test the purity and weight of metals.</term> who cleanse perfect metals from the imperfect through the **ash-cupel** <term>ash-cupel: From "cineritium cupellæ," a shallow porous cup used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead and impurities by heating.</term>, they separate the true from the false. They weigh them on the balance of **Experience** and **Reason** which agrees with it, so that the good is not rejected with the bad, and the bad does not come into use with the good and disturb one's judgment. 

Such figures have shone forth in every age in the arts and in all faculties; the age of the fathers showed this in Theology and Medicine, just as our own age has in Astronomy and many other sciences. Among these, the most eminent place is claimed by those who are said to have established the Order of German blood, the **R. C.** <note>The Rosicrucians (Rose Cross), a secret brotherhood of sages and alchemists who claimed to possess a "Universal Reformation of Mankind."</note>. According to these, it is necessary to direct the rules of practice toward the goal of each science, so that a **Harmony** <term>Harmony: From "Harmonia," the concept that all branches of knowledge and all parts of the universe are interconnected in a perfect, divine proportion.</term> of all and individual arts and sciences might not only be found, but also...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the axioms or fundamental truths discovered by the R.C. (Rosicrucian) order, transitioning into a philosophical defense of how these sciences harmonize to reveal the secrets of Nature and the purpose of human existence.</meta>

[of each art] its own end can be investigated and obtained through itself. What **Plato** <note>Plato (c. 427–348 BC), the Greek philosopher whose ideas on the harmony of the cosmos were central to Renaissance "natural science."</note> pronounced regarding the harmony of good things and the consensus of minds—that neither the **Evening Star** <note>original: "Hesperum"</note> nor the **Morning Star** <note>original: "Luciferum"</note> shines more brightly—should, I believe, be rightly said of the symphony or natural embrace of these arts. For what is more suited to demonstrating their truth? What is more appropriate for the benefit of humanity? What is more magnificent for our necessity? 

Through these arts, silent teachers speak and understand one another. Through them, **Nature** opens the hidden recesses of her heart and reveals them only to the worthy. Through these same means, she, the mother, offers herself to be touched and seen by her children; that is, Nature reveals herself to herself. Or rather, they allow **man**—who is not merely a part of Nature’s works, but a condensed **Idea** <term>Idea: Referring to the "Microcosm," the philosophical concept that a human being is a miniature map or reflection of the entire universe.</term> of the entire universe—to know and contemplate himself: to what end he was created, why he was placed into this world, and how he shall eventually depart from here into another life.

Therefore, we owe the greatest gratitude to the seekers of this **natural science** <note>original: "naturalis scientiæ"</note>, whoever they may be, or in whatever place they choose to keep themselves hidden and safe. We should offer them thanks, not the insults, slanders, or infamy that are the **prizes** <term>prizes: From the Greek "braveia" (βραβεῖα), meaning the rewards or trophies given in a contest.</term> of that **many-headed beast** <note>A common historical metaphor for the fickle, uneducated, and often judgmental common public.</note>, the common crowd.

# CHAPTER THREE.

*That among the secrets of the German **Fraternity of the R.C.** <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis), a legendary secret society of the 17th century claiming hidden wisdom in alchemy and medicine.</note> is a **Universal Medicine** <term>Universal Medicine: Also known as the Elixir of Life or the Panacea, this was a legendary substance in alchemy believed to cure all diseases and prolong life indefinitely.</term>, a chief Gift of God after the knowledge of Himself.*

Under the name of **Secrets** <note>original: "Arcanorum"; refers to hidden or "arcane" knowledge that is not accessible to the uninitiated.</note>, lest anyone should think that the inventions or fantasies of just anyone are included—things which find a place neither in nature nor in Experience—we understand only the hidden works of potential nature, which can be brought into action by natural means. From these, we exclude and banish **magical** <note>In this context, the author likely refers to "black magic" or sorcery, as opposed to "natural magic" which was seen as early science.</note>, **necromantic** <note>The practice of communicating with the dead or using demonic influence.</note>, and diabolical arts, as well as the dreams devised by men which have "neither head nor tail" <note>original: "nec caput nec caudam habent"; a classical idiom meaning something that lacks logic, order, or a clear beginning and end.</note>. That is to say, we reject things which show no signs imprinted by nature. For those things which nature, like a mother, did not produce, she is recognized instead as their stepmother; she does not bestow the gifts of her benevolence upon them, nor does she provide milk or nourishment. Whence there is no hope for success or growth, just as with foreign or substituted <note>original: "supposititiis"; the text cuts off here, referring to "supposititious" or counterfeit children/objects.</note>

<summary>The author defines "secrets" (Arcana) as hidden natural processes that can be scientifically realized, explicitly distancing Rosicrucian knowledge from superstition, black magic, and illogical fantasies.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucianism, Universal Medicine, Arcana, Natural Philosophy, Alchemy vs. Magic, Experience</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 24 regarding the relationship between Nature and Art (human skill/science). with counterfeit offspring <note>original: "supposititiis fætib."; completing the word from the previous page. The author is discussing things that appear natural but are actually false or artificial.</note> by nature's nod: The intricate art of many-sided things <note>original: "Polij dædala ars"; referring to the multifaceted skill of human craftsmanship, invoking Daedalus, the mythical Greek inventor.</note> or human curiosity has discovered many things that nature does not permit. Nor is it right for the former to imitate the latter, but on the contrary, since **Nature is the Mistress and Art is the Handmaid**, and the latter is subjected to the power of the former; although the mistress does not reject the faithful advice and services of the handmaid. We know that the **German Society** <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood.</note> has received both mistress and handmaid into its hospitality, and has assigned to each its own honor; that is, it has treated nature not without art, nor art without nature, but has joined **Practice** <note>original: "Praxin"</note> to **Theory** and action to potential. 

For how would the certainty of the sciences have been grasped if the work, which is the goal of art, had not demonstrated it? How would the precepts of art have been found to conform to principles and the end result if the **Theoretical** part of natural philosophy had not harmonized and fitted them together? Indeed, just as the right hand needs the help of the left and vice versa, so practice needs theory. 

In particular, we are not ignorant that the gift of the **gold-bearing art** <term>gold-bearing art: "Auriferæ artis," or alchemy, specifically the transmutation of base metals into gold.</term> was divinely granted to the said **Brothers of the R.C.** among the secrets most useful to the human race. How remarkable a work this is, of both art and nature, can scarcely be expressed by the words of men. The world itself, whose mouth is every corner, hu- <note>The text cuts off here, continuing to the word "human" on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "gold-bearing art" (alchemy) from page 25. The catchword "captu" from the previous page likely combined with the opening of this page to form "cuius" (whose), referring back to the dignity and value of the alchemical art.</meta>

...whose dignity and preciousness it attests and proclaims, although this is unknown neither to the blind nor the deaf: Here is the **kernel** hidden within the wrinkled and hard shell of the **almond** <note>original: "amygdali"; the almond often symbolizes the "kernel" of truth or the hidden essence within a rough exterior in alchemical and mystical traditions</note>, namely the end and fruit of the tree, and the beginning or seed of the same. This is the circle which **Nature** makes by folding the end into the beginning and the beginning into the end <note>A reference to the Ouroboros or the cyclical nature of the Great Work in alchemy.</note>.

This gift is so great that it turns many into stone—just as the head of the **Gorgon** <note>Medusa, whose severed head was used as a weapon to petrify enemies.</note> was given by **Perseus** to **Pallas** <note>Pallas Athena/Minerva; she placed the head on her shield, the Aegis.</note>—that is, it reduces them to a stupor and a stony hardness upon which scarcely anything can be inscribed <note>The author suggests that the sheer magnitude of this divine secret paralyses the minds of those who cannot comprehend it.</note>.

If a great **treasure** <note>original: "thesaurus"</note> were given to anyone by a prince, hardly anyone would be found so ill-disposed toward that prince as to say that such a treasure or amount of money could not be given, or that it does not exist in the nature of things. Or, if he dared to say this, he would expect to find himself in very poor favor with the giver. 

Now, however, when we say that such a gift has been granted to some among men by **God** or His minister and Treasurer, **Nature**, behold! there are those who, without shame, clamor against the one who affirms it. They accuse him of lying, they deny the treasure of Nature, and they [measure] the grace and most bountiful hand of God by their own <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the author is likely about to say they measure God's power by their own limited understanding or narrow-mindedness.</note>...

<summary>The author uses the metaphors of the almond's kernel and the Gorgon's head to describe the hidden, overwhelming nature of alchemical secrets. He argues that while people readily accept the generosity of earthly princes, they foolishly doubt the even greater treasures God provides through Nature.</summary>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against skeptics who doubt the reality of alchemical gifts. It follows the previous page's discussion on how the magnitude of these secrets can "petrify" or overwhelm the unprepared mind.</meta>

...limit and confine it by their mental grasp; as if God himself were as powerless to give as they are to believe, or as if Nature were as emptied of every gift as they are of intellectual capacity. And these people do not doubt they have fulfilled their duty if they strenuously deny every word of others and, like spiders’ webs, entangle and catch various "texts" <note>original: "textus"; the author uses the metaphor of insects caught in a web to describe how critics trap and pick apart the writings of alchemists</note> (which are produced by the **alchemists** <term>Chymicis: practitioners of the alchemical art, seeking the transformation of matter and spirit</term> to support their cause). In doing so, they are impious toward **God the Creator** and ungrateful toward **Mother Nature**.

For they are like mules who kick their mother with their heels, or like **cuckoos** who devour the bird that raised them. Many also imitate the **Aesopian fox**, who devises marvelous tricks to snatch the cheese from the crow’s mouth by calling the bird "melodious" when it is anything but. But who would be moved by such praises or insults, such that they would choose to speak or keep silent at the whim of a malicious person? Alchemical practitioners are not children who often trade a gold coin for an apple, or swap the most precious things in their hands for mere rattles. 

Instead, they know—if anyone does—what it means to truly live, and how much "real money" differs from "play money" <note>original: "æra lupinis"; lupines (a type of bean) were often used as stage money or gaming counters, contrasting with actual copper coins</note>, or gold from bronze, lest they be judged like **Glaucus** <note>A reference to the Homeric story from the Iliad where Glaucus foolishly traded his golden armor for the bronze armor of Diomedes.</note>. This was acknowledged by **Bacasser** <note>A philosopher appearing in the "Turba Philosophorum" (Assembly of the Philosophers), one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between various sages.</note> in the *Assembly of Philosophers*, who said: "What you seek is by no means of small price: **For you seek the greatest treasure and the most excellent gift of God.**" And this is, without doubt, most true.

<summary>The author defends alchemists against skeptical critics, comparing detractors to ungrateful animals or the trickster fox of Aesop. He asserts that true alchemists are wise enough to value the "divine gift" of their art above mere trifles, citing the authority of the Turba Philosophorum.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, skepticism, Mother Nature, Aesop's fables, Glaucus, Turba Philosophorum, Bacasser, divine gift, intellectual capacity</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought from the previous page regarding the value of alchemical knowledge, completing the comparison to the foolishness of Glaucus (who traded gold for bronze) and transitioning into the proper use of wealth and the goals of the "Brothers."</meta>

...is; for in the **sublunary region** <term>sublunaris regio: in the traditional worldview, this is the earthly realm below the moon, characterized by change, decay, and the four elements</term>, there is nothing more precious than that gift, after the **knowledge of God** <note>original: "Dei agnitionem"</note>, whether we consider the medicine of the human body or of metals. Riches are placed after virtue by true **Philosophers** <term>Philosophis: here referring to alchemical sages or seekers of universal truth</term>, but in truth the world is ruled by these [riches], as if by the deputies of all worldly things. Virtue, nobility, beauty, honor, courage, strength, learning, and all other things that acknowledge the title of "good" yield to them: so much so that if you have called a man rich, it is enough to have remained silent about his other qualities.

But just as it is not fitting for one who studies wisdom to be devoted solely to accumulating riches, so neither do the aforementioned **Brothers** <note>The "Brothers" refers to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis), whose manifestos at this time claimed they possessed the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone but valued spiritual wisdom above gold</note> desire only this **Stone** <term>lapidem: the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance of the "Great Work" capable of transmuting metals and lengthening life</term>, but they insist upon the contemplation of the whole of nature. Since that stream [the Stone] is a rivulet from nature's fountain—and richer even than the **Ganges** <note>The Ganges river was famously associated in antiquity and the Renaissance with golden sands and immense wealth</note>—there is no reason why they should pass it by untasted, or, following the example of **Crates of Thebes** <note>Crates (c. 365–285 BCE) was a Cynic philosopher who famously renounced his large fortune, throwing his money into the sea to ensure it would not distract him from philosophy</note>, throw well-possessed gold into the sea. For with such wealth, so many alms can be given, so many secrets investigated, and so many works perfected, all of which may result in the **glory of God** and the benefit of one’s neighbor.

# CHAPTER FOUR.

*That such a medicine is hidden in Nature is evident from demonstrations.*

But many will ask: "What kind of medicine are you singing of to me, besides the **dogmatic** <term>dogmaticam: refers to traditional medicine based on established authorities like Galen, as opposed to the "hermetic" or chemical medicine the author proposes</term> variety?" To these people, who are so unskilled in all matters, I would prefer to satisfy them with silence rather than press upon them more **notions** <term>noëmata: from the Greek word for "thoughts" or "concepts"</term> previously unknown to them, thus creating the excessive burden of learning things never heard before. I would rather remain silent than produce the weight of these matters, which they perhaps oppose and hate worse than a dog or a snake <note>original: "cane peius & angue"; a classical idiom for something loathed or avoided at all costs</note>.

Truly, we take **Medicine** in this context to mean a medicament—whatever kind it may be—whether it is suited to a single disease, or is a **Polychreston** <term>Polychreston: a remedy "of many uses"</term>, or a **Panchreston** <term>Panchreston: a universal remedy or "cure-all," from the Greek for "useful for everything"</term> and universal. It is all the same whether it is called so in an absolute sense or only in a relative sense; therefore, the **medical art** <note>original: "ars medica"</note> suffers no prejudice. Indeed—

<summary>The author introduces the concept of a hidden, universal medicine found in Nature, distinguishing it from traditional "dogmatic" medicine while addressing the skepticism of those unfamiliar with alchemical concepts.</summary>
<keywords>hidden medicine, dogmatic medicine, panacea, Polychreston, Panchreston, medical art, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter Four. The catchword "conce-" from the previous page aligns perfectly with the first word of this page, "concedimus" (we concede), bridging the transition from the definition of medicine to its alchemical applications.</meta>

we concede that this **Medicament** <term>Medicamentum: a medicinal substance or remedy</term> is an accessory to **Dogmatic Medicine** <term>Dogmaticæ Medicinæ: traditional medical practice based on the established theories of authorities like Galen</term>, nor do we ever separate it from that field insofar as it is applied to curing a sick human being. If, however, it is found to be endowed by a certain privilege of nature for the use of metals, this should not be envied, but rather acknowledged with a grateful mind. 

There are those who promise great medicines from the most <unclear>base</unclear> parts and liquids of the earth, such as dung, animal urine, furnace mud, bricks, and other things of this kind. We do not contradict them, since there is scarcely any physical thing that presents itself from which some **medical secret** <term>arcani medici: a hidden, powerful essence or "arcanum" believed by alchemists to hold curative properties</term> is not thought to emerge. 

Who, therefore, will be of so stubborn a mind or so perverse a will as to deny that from the most precious of all metals, or from its active part—such as the **Quintessence** <term>Quinta essentia: the "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated medicinal part of a substance</term>—the most effective Medicine can be drawn? Or who would deny that the **Gold-making Tincture** <term>Tinctura aurifica: an alchemical substance capable of transmuting metals and acting as a universal panacea</term> is the safest Medicament for the diseases of the human body? 

Certain people immoderately praise a "fabulous stone," formed in the sands, for the consolidation of bones because of its power of drying and cooling; and the **Philosophical** [stone] on the con- <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "tra," likely for "econtra" (on the contrary).</note>

<summary>The author argues that while alchemical medicine is a supplement to traditional medicine, it is superior because it is derived from "noble" metals rather than waste products. He asserts that if nature provides healing secrets in dung and urine, it must surely provide more powerful ones in gold and the Quintessence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument against those who reject alchemical medicine. ...against, for no other reason than because this [alchemical medicine] is hidden from them, while the other is better known; they strive to eliminate it from the nature of things. But just as **Lycurgus** <note>original: "Lycurgus"; referring to the mythical King of Thrace who, in his hatred for Dionysus and the abuse of wine, attempted to destroy all grapevines but accidentally struck his own legs with an axe</note>, who had decided to cut down the vines because of the abuse of wine, instead cut off his own legs, so these men will prove their own reason to be blind and their **Experience** <term>Experientiam: practical knowledge gained through trial and observation, often contrasted with theoretical "Reason"</term> to be utterly crude, before they can ever demonstrate that the testimonies of so many most learned men of every age and land, and the **gold-born Symbols** <term>Symbola aurigena: alchemical signs or emblems believed to contain the "seeds" of truth regarding the creation of gold</term>, are devoid of truth.

If anyone does not allow the consensus of the **twelve nations** <note>A reference to the structure of Maier's previous work, where he cites alchemical authorities from twelve different cultures (including the Egyptians, Arabs, Germans, etc.)</note> and ages to satisfy him, I shall suggest nothing to him except that he remain in his own disbelief, and imitate that "greatest among the great" who declared that he neither wanted nor was able to believe it, even if he were to see it happen or had done it himself. But if they are so stubborn and **stiff-necked** <note>original: "duræ ceruicis"; a biblical idiom for those who are obstinate and refuse to change their minds</note> in other matters as well, then no craftsman [will...] for them, but if—

<summary>Maier argues that skeptics who reject alchemical medicine are as self-destructive as Lycurgus. He refuses to repeat the extensive proofs already published in his book 'The Golden Table,' noting that some people remain obstinate even when faced with miracles or direct experience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a critique of those who stubbornly reject alchemical truths. ...themselves, as **Aspendius** <note>original: "Aspendius"; refers to the "Aspendian harper" of antiquity who, out of selfishness or ego, played his music so quietly that only he could hear it</note> once sang, lest they perhaps fall into a swoon of the mind if they should perceive something to be true that is contrary to their own opinion. As for us, I believe we should be excused from the task of trying to cure them.

However, that such a medicine exists in the depths of nature is first proven by the **Experience** <term>Experientia: practical trial and observation, the "gold standard" for alchemists over mere theory</term> of countless individuals who possessed the true powders. With these powders, they performed the **transmutations** <term>Transmutatio: the alchemical process of changing "base" or "imperfect" metals into noble ones</term> of imperfect metals and **Mercury** <term>Mercurius: quicksilver, considered the "mother" of all metals in alchemy</term> into **Gold**, to the amazement of those standing by—even if those who performed it were not true masters of the craft, or perhaps had not acquired the powders by a just title. For those who have actually accomplished this themselves very rarely resort to ostentation...

<summary>Maier mocks skeptics who refuse to believe in alchemy or modern astronomy, comparing their ignorance to ancient flat-earth theories. He asserts that the reality of transmutation is proven by the successful experiences of many, even those who are not master alchemists.</summary>

33.
B
<meta>catchword: quibus</meta>

[reaso]n; they use it for themselves, nor do they easily grant it to others, unless prevented by death or compelled by force. Secondly, reason supports Experience. Although reason may seem superfluous to those who have seen with their own eyes—since it is pointless to prove through logic what can enter through the senses—nevertheless, the senses are not always moved by their own true object. This is especially true in this desperate matter and "form of the art" <note>The "form of the art" refers to the specific alchemical process or the "soul" of the metal being transformed</note> which is not to be shared with others. Therefore, a logical argument must be submitted as follows: 

If a gold-making power <term>vis aurificans</term> <note>The inherent ability or "seed" within a substance to produce gold</note> can be held in a natural subject as its **form**; and if the **matter** <term>materia</term> of **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term>—whether the common kind or that of "imperfect" metals <note>Alchemists believed metals like lead or tin were "imperfect" versions of gold that could be purified</note>—can be so cleansed that the matter becomes natural; and finally, if this matter and form can be joined in the proper weights with a moderate, not violent, **efficient cause** <note>The "efficient cause" in this context is usually the controlled heat of the alchemical furnace</note>, and be nurtured so that one does not exceed or destroy the other, but both are moved by a reciprocal motion toward where the **formal power** inclines; then it is impossible but that a gold-making power be produced in the matter. From this, a **form** is created, and once it is possessed, gold is generated from non-gold—that is, from quicksilver, which has been brought into balance by that form. 

But all these first premises are true: they can be possessed, joined, nurtured, and moved in this way. Therefore, the conclusion is also true. The necessity of this reasoning is sought from the **four types of causes** <note>Referring to Aristotle's four causes: material, formal, efficient, and final, which were the standard framework for scientific explanation in the Renaissance</note>.

<summary>The author presents a formal logical argument (a syllogism) to prove that alchemy is possible by refining quicksilver into gold through the balance of matter, form, and heat. He asserts that while eyewitness experience is valuable, reason is necessary to explain the underlying natural causes of transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>vis aurificans, alchemy, quicksilver, four causes, Aristotle, transmutation, matter and form, logic</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 33, where the author established the logical framework for the "gold-making power." The catchword "quibus" (by which) from the previous page begins the opening sentence here, linking the joining of matter and form to the resulting effect.</meta>

When these have been joined by art or nature, as is fitting, that which ought to be made from them arises in both cases. Nor is there a difference in the effect if there is no distinction in the causes. And from this, the **Major premise** is true and evident by its own light. Therefore, the entire burden of proof rests on the **minor premise** or the **assumption**. 

This assumption has six parts, the first of which is: a **gold-making power** <term>vis aurificans</term> <note>original: "Vis aurificans"; the innate potential or "seed" within a substance to transform into gold</note> can be held in a natural subject. The reason is that we know the **matter** <term>materia</term> closest to gold, which nature uses in the generation of gold, and which nature would use if it could produce the **tincture** <term>tinctura</term> <note>The "tincture" is the elusive alchemical agent or "philosopher's stone" in liquid form, capable of staining or "dyeing" base metals into gold</note> without the hands of an artisan. If anyone denies this, I appeal to the "cradles of gold," the nature of which I have sufficiently described in the *Viatorium* <note>Likely referring to Michael Maier’s "Viatorium, hoc est, De Montibus Planetarum," an alchemical travelogue</note>, under the "mountain of the seventh planet" <note>The seventh planet is Saturn, which in alchemy corresponds to the metal lead, often seen as the starting point or "mountain" from which gold is sought</note> and in other places. 

The second part is: that the matter of common **quicksilver** <term>argentum vivum</term> <note>Mercury</note> and that of the "imperfect" metals <note>Metals like lead, tin, or copper, which alchemists believed were "diseased" or unfinished versions of gold</note> can be so cleansed that it becomes equivalent to natural matter—or rather, it becomes that very thing. If anyone is skeptical of this, let him consult **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath; his Latin works were the "textbooks" of medieval and Renaissance purification techniques</note>, who does almost nothing else but purify the impure, wash away the dregs, separate with water and fire, **sublime** <term>sublimatio</term> the pure, and through **distillation** <term>distillatio</term> separate it from **heterogeneous** <note>original: "heterogeneis"; substances that are foreign or different in kind from the pure metal</note> elements. There is, moreover, a method favored by Geber, which he nevertheless hides and does not hand down literally,

...has shown, except sparsely. Third, there is required a joining of **matter and form** under the proper weights: **This is the work, this the labor** <note>original: "Hoc opus, hic labor"; a famous quotation from Virgil’s *Aeneid*, emphasizing that while descending into the underworld is easy, the true challenge is the climb back out—here referring to the difficulty of achieving perfect proportions</note>. Here, the factor of weight increases the difficulty. Yet, a solution is possible to find, since the philosophers suggest it and experience demonstrates it even to the ignorant.

The fourth requirement is a moderate and non-violent **efficient cause**, which is a fire—both a **fire against nature** <term>ignis contra naturam</term> <note>A technical alchemical term for a chemical solvent or agent that "burns" or decomposes substances without literal flames</note> and an elemental fire, which can be obtained in a similar way. Fifth, these substances can be fostered so that one does not exceed or destroy the other, just as eggs are fostered by a hen through incubation, or as is done by **artifice in Egypt** <note>A reference to the famous Egyptian egg-ovens (*mamals*), which alchemists often cited as the historical precedent for the "athanor" or slow-heating furnace</note>. Sixth, the matter must be moved with a reciprocal motion, like a chick in an egg or a **wood-worm** <term>Cnips</term> <note>original: "Cnips"; a Greek-derived term for a tiny wood-boring insect or larva</note> in wood, always striving to change its place. Since all these conditions can coincide through the proper arrangement of the artisan...

## CHAPTER FIVE.

*It is well known that in ancient times among other nations there were **Fraternities** or **Philosophical Colleges** which uniquely cherished the secrets of nature and this **Medicine** <note>In alchemical literature, "Medicine" usually refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir, which was thought to "cure" base metals of their impurities to become gold, or to restore human health</note>, and as if passing it from hand to hand through many centuries, they left these secrets to successors selected from the entire number of Philosophers.*

Besides those who expressly deny the art, there are others who do not so much [deny] this [art], by which...

<summary>The author concludes his logical defense of alchemy by dismissing stubborn skeptics and begins a historical account of ancient secret societies and "Philosophical Colleges" that preserved alchemical secrets. He emphasizes that this knowledge was passed down through a selective chain of succession over many centuries.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, philosophical colleges, fraternities, transmission of knowledge, the Medicine, secrets of nature, succession, skeptics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical defense of alchemy to the historical evidence of secret societies. The previous page ended with the observation that some people accept alchemy as a possible science but deny that a secret fraternity (like the Rosicrucians) exists to guard it. ...they attack the **Fraternity**, while granting that the **art** <note>The "art" refers to alchemy or the Hermetic sciences</note> is possible; yet they by no means believe that such a society lies hidden anywhere. Since these people should not feel ashamed to be taught, or at least to be advised, we must recall to memory similar examples which occurred in ancient times and can be compared to the **Fraternity** of our own age. I speak of **Colleges**—secret gatherings of similar craftsmanship and natural matters, with a profession that evokes no small wonder—which once existed in various places and nations. Although we have explained these extensively in our own works, *The Most Secret of Secrets* <note>original: "Arcanis Arcanissimis"; a reference to Maier’s book *Arcana Arcanissima* (1614)</note> or *Hieroglyphics* <note>original: "Hieroglypicis"</note> throughout books 1 and 4, as well as especially in the third book of *Symbols* <note>original: "Symbolis"; likely referring to his work *Symbola Aureae Mensae* (1617)</note>, we will nevertheless run through them here briefly, so they do not slip from the minds of the uninformed.

## *1. The College, the most ancient of the Egyptians.*

Among the Egyptians, it is established by a thousand signs that there was a **College** of **Philosophers**, **Priests**, and **Kings**. For the Religion of all Egypt, as well as the governance of the kingdom, was shaped according to the laws and instructions of the **Philosophers** under the name of **Osiris** and **Isi-** <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "dis," completing the name "Isis." In alchemical lore, Osiris and Isis often represent the dual principles of nature—the sun and moon, or the fixed and the volatile—whose union produces the Great Work.</note>

<summary>The author argues against skeptics of secret societies by pointing to historical precedents of "Philosophical Colleges," beginning with the ancient Egyptians who integrated science, religion, and governance under the figures of Osiris and Isis.</summary>

<keywords>Fraternity, secret societies, alchemy, Egyptian colleges, Osiris, Isis, Michael Maier, Arcana Arcanissima, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Egyptian "College" of philosophers, specifically focusing on how their mythology—Isis, Osiris, and Mercury—served as a coded language for alchemical principles. It completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the goddess Isis.</meta>

-dis <note>Completing the name "Isidis" or Isis from the previous page</note>, whom they imagined as Gods and Egyptian kings, though they are nothing other than chemical subjects <note>The "subjects" of alchemy are the raw materials or base metals used in the Work</note>, otherwise called the Sun and Moon, Sulfur and Quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; this refers to Mercury, the metallic element</note>. Isis furnished Egypt with laws according to the counsel of Mercury, which Mercury had received from Vulcan, by whom he had also been instructed. Of all the fields of Egypt, a third part first went to the priests, the second to the kings, and the third to the people. From this the antiquity of the priesthood is established, for they began at the same time as the kingdom. In the first place, however, they served Vulcan, who had a most splendid temple there, roofed with gold and containing golden chambers, in which the bull Apis <note>original: "Apis bos"</note> was worshiped for external ceremony. He was black over his whole body, except that he had a white crescent moon on his forehead or on one of his sides. This bull represented Osiris: for just as Osiris is the Sun, Sulfur, and the Soul <note>original: "anima"</note> which migrates into other imperfect bodies, so he was imagined to pass into this bull. After a space of four years, when the bull was submerged in a sacred spring and buried (wherefore he was called Serapis), the task of searching for another of similar color throughout all Egypt fell upon the priests. During this intervening time, all things were full of mourning, wailing, and grief, until a new bull was found and re- <note>The catchword "ductus" on the next page completes the word "reductus," meaning "led back" or "returned"</note>

<summary>This page explains that Egyptian gods like Isis and Osiris were actually alchemical symbols for Sulfur and Mercury, and describes the ritual worship and cycle of the Apis bull as an allegory for the movement of the "Soul" through matter.</summary>

By these, holy or *Philosophical* <note>In this context, "philosophical" refers specifically to alchemical secrets</note> matters were recorded; by the others, any other or *profane* matters. The *Hieroglyphics* gave the common people a reason for worshiping those animals whose figures they saw were included among the sacred letters, though they did not understand them. This is the origin of the *Egyptian* superstition, which other nations and almost the entire world followed. From these letters, the *Phoenix* is a bird, a mere...

In these figures, Anubis with a dog's head, called the "barker," denotes Mercury, who is called Thoth <note>original: "Theut"</note>. The Egyptians also had twelve gods, acting as auxiliary deities, through which they expressed various allegories of their secret art, so that the common people <note>original: "vulgus"</note> would not perceive them as anything other than gods. Instructed in these matters, the priests privy to the gold-making art <term>artis aurigenæ; from the Latin "aurum" (gold) and "gigno" (to produce)</term> had a thousand reasons for concealing it and performing their sacred rites. For this reason, it was <gloss>n</gloss>known to none among the common folk of the ancients that such an art existed in the nature of things, much less that it was nurtured in the heart of Egypt.

However, in this college, not all were priests, nor were all the philosophers—who were the sons of the priests—nor all the kings—who were chosen from the priests—but a select few chosen from the whole number. These men celebrated their secret meetings and dealt with the marrow <note>original: "medulla"; the inner essence or "pith" of the secret</note> of their sacred rites, while everyone else was occupied with the shells <note>original: "cortices"; the outer layers or literal religious ceremonies</note>. These individuals alone understood what was meant by Apis, Osiris, Isis, Typhon, Anubis or Thoth, and Mercury (whom the Greeks call Hermes), and what was meant by Horus. Furthermore, they understood the ceremonies and allegories established around them. These same people not only read the hieroglyphics, but
