It was for this reason that Leo, the chief of the Egyptian priests, revealed to Alexander the Great as a secret of the world <term>secreto mundi; a profound mystery concerning the nature of reality</term> that those gods whom the Egyptians worshipped are not, and never were, gods, but were in fact men. For the priest was not permitted to speak of the matter as it truly was <note>the author implies the "human" explanation was a screen to hide even deeper alchemical secrets</note>, lest he be forced by the King to reveal everything. 

Yet Alexander wrote of this secret—which was then scarcely believable to Greece and the whole pagan world—to his mother, Olympias. He ordered that the letter be burned immediately after it was read, so that it would not become a scandal to the whole kingdom and the entire populace. For the people were kept in their duty under required oaths, bound by the fear and worship of these Deities <term>Numinum; divine powers or wills</term>—even if they were false and merely presupposed—just as if they were true. More concerning this college may be seen in the places already mentioned.

## *II. The College of the Eumolpidae of Eleusis.*

It is clear from what has been said previously that the Egyptians were the primary authors of all pagan religion and false theology <note>original: "pseudotheologæ"; the author uses this to describe the literal or external religious myths which hide deeper truths</note>. From these Egyptians, Orpheus borrowed the secret art <note>original: "artem occultam"</note> along with their allegorical gods, and established a new religion in Greece, once he had introduced the festival and ceremonies of Dionysus <note>the Greek god of wine, fertility, and theater, often associated with transformative spiritual states</note> to the Thebans in Boeotia. 

From Orpheus, the first high priest of all the pagans, the Greek poets took the subject matter for their poems concerning the gods, the heroes, and their deeds, as well as the opportunity to pile falsehood upon falsehood. He was followed by Linus, Musaeus, Amphion, Hesiod, and Homer, who is the great father, teacher, and leader <term>antesignanus; literally a "standard-bearer" who walks in front of the troops</term> of both the Greek and Latin poets. 

However, setting these figures aside, we shall consider Eumolpus <note>a legendary figure in Greek mythology, the supposed founder of the Eleusinian Mysteries</note>, an Egyptian by birth who lived in Athens. Being descended from the aforementioned priests, he established a kingdom in Attica and organized a Philosophical College in Eleusis <note>Eleusis was the center of the "Eleusinian Mysteries," the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece</note> under these laws: 

1. That a meeting should be held on a fixed day every year...

Moreover, the Eumolpid High Priest had a golden key fitted to his tongue, as a sign of silence. From these things, one can reasonably conclude the purpose for the founding of this College. This they conducted with the utmost secrecy, lest its very name become known to anyone else. 

This art is demonstrated by those four images: that of the Creator, who is the giver and first cause; the Sun and the Moon, representing form and matter; and Mercury, the water of solution <note>the "mercurial water" or solvent used in alchemical dissolution</note>, without which the work is not perfected. 

In addition to the aforementioned ceremonies, certain allegorical verses were sung during those sacred rites, which figuratively encompassed the entire operation. These were understood only by those "in the know" <note>original: "consciis"; those sharing the secret knowledge</note>, while others accepted them as obscure oracles. This was their meaning: 

*The Dragon became the parent of the Bull, and the Bull in turn the parent of the Dragon. Ceres gave birth to the maiden Perephatte <note>original: "Perephatten"; a name for Persephone, daughter of Demeter/Ceres</note>, with whom Jupiter—who had fathered her—was joined after transforming into a Dragon. Perephatte then gave birth to a bull-shaped son. For this reason, certain poets praise the Bull as the father of the Dragon, and the Dragon, again, as the father of the Bull; and leading these secret deeds up the mountain, they celebrated with a shepherd's staff.* 

It is manifest that these things are found only in alchemical matters <note>original: "chymicis"</note>, where the Bull, which represents the Sun, and the Dragon, which represents the Moon, lie together;

But for this reason, the Goddess, becoming angry, killed the father and gifted Triptolemus a chariot drawn by winged dragons, with which he might teach mortals the sowing of crops. That these are purely **alchemical** <term>chymica; here referring to the "spagyric" art of refining substances through fire and maturation</term> institutions, we have most abundantly demonstrated elsewhere; therefore, we are more brief here.

The reason why these gods were not to be named is obvious: namely, so that they would not be disclosed to the common people, just like the Eleusinian Mysteries <note>the most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece</note>, with which they agree in their purpose and manner of instruction. ## *IV. There was a College of Magi in Persia, or of Chaldeans in Babylonia.*

Among the Persians, there was such great esteem for <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magiae"; here understood as natural science rather than sorcery</note>—that is, the science of natural things—that according to the witness of Tullius <note>the Roman orator Cicero</note> in his book *On Divination*, no one was admitted to the kingship unless they had first learned it. Ad-

<keywords>Proserpina, Pluto, Ceres, nigredo, alchemical symbolism, Triptolemus, Persian Magi, Cicero, natural science</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Magi. The catchword "Ad-" from the previous page completes the phrase "Ad hanc cognofcendam," meaning "To understand this [Magic]..."</meta>

To understand this [science], the Greeks Pythagoras, Empedocles, Plato, and others sailed across the sea; upon their return (as Pliny says in book 30 <note>Pliny the Elder, *Natural History*, Book XXX, which provides a history of magic</note>), they preached it and held it among their secret mysteries. They say its author was Zoroaster, and following him were the Medes Apuscorus and Zaratus, the Babylonian Marmarides, the Arab Hippocus, and the Assyrian Zarmocenides. Hermippus <note>a Greek biographer of the 3rd century BCE</note> writes that Zoroaster lived on cheese in the forest for twenty years, where he devoted himself to the study of <term>Magic</term> <note>original: "Magiæ"; here referring to the study of natural laws and the stars</note>. From this it appears that he was dedicated more to the contemplation of nature than to illicit and <term>Necromantic</term> <note>dark rituals involving communication with the dead</note> consecrations. Xanthus the Lydian <note>a 5th-century BCE historian</note> asserts that Zoroaster lived only six hundred years before Xerxes’ invasion of Greece. However, it is established that he lived in the time of Ninus <note>the mythical founder of Nineveh</note>, by whom he was driven from the kingdom of the Bactrians; this was the same time that Abraham was born, who preceded the first Olympiad by 1,494 years. And Moses lived a little more than six hundred years after Zoroaster. Some judge that Zoroaster was the one who is called Mizraim <note>the Hebrew name for Egypt and a descendant of Noah</note> in the Holy Scriptures, or his son, the grandson of Ham <note>original: "Chami"</note> (although others assert it was Ham himself), who practiced the Magic art with wonderful efficacy: for [he knew] the skill of the stars...

he knew [the skill of the stars] most perfectly, and he asserted that from them all human and lower things are ruled and nourished. From this, he received his name, as if <note>original Greek: ζῶον ἄστρον (*zoon astron*)</note> "living star," whereas he was previously called Zarades. This magical doctrine flourished ever after among the Persians, and especially among the Assyrians, who are called <term>Chaldeans</term> <note>an ancient people of Mesopotamia famous for their knowledge of astronomy and astrology</note>. Being skilled in the knowledge of the stars, they were accustomed to turn away evils from men with sacred chants, and to explain dreams and omens with reliable interpretation. They maintained a college in Babylon for many centuries. Apollonius of Tyana <note>a famous Greek philosopher and reputed wonder-worker of the 1st century CE</note> traveled to them and conferred seriously with them daily regarding abstruse and secret matters at midday and midnight. There he was received as a friend by King Bardanes <note>Vardanes I, a King of the Parthian Empire</note> and held in honor, and was provided by the same king with everything necessary for his journey.

# V. The College of Brahmans
## in India.

The wise men of India had a city in a pleasant, open region, fertile with every kind of fruit; it was possessed only by them and governed by their own laws—

[go]verned. In the time of Apollonius, there were eighteen of these men, under Hiarchas <note>also known as Iarchas, the leader of the Indian Brahmans</note> their leader. They possessed a well four paces in width, by whose divine power the Indians were accustomed to swear an inviolable oath. Near the well was a crater of great capacity, full of fire, standing upright; its flame, which was of a leaden color and produced no smoke, did not rise above the edges of the crater. On either side, two jars were raised up—one of the winds, the other of the rains. When the first was opened, the winds blew out with great force; when the other was opened, mists emerged, moistening the air and bringing forth rain, providing remedies for excessive dryness and humidity.

Hiarchas predicted to Apollonius everything that had happened to him on his journey, having embraced the doctrine of <term>Metempsychosis</term> <note>original: *Metapsychosin*; the philosophical belief in the transmigration of souls or reincarnation</note> with him. These wise men also, while Apollonius was present, cast out a demon from a certain youth, and they healed the lame, the one-eyed, and a woman suffering from <gloss>recurrent</gloss> miscarriage. Iarchas gave him seven rings, marked with the symbols of the seven stars <note>referring to the seven classical planets: the Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note>, whose engraved characters showed the effects of wonderful things. They also conferred with him regarding the **Marticora** beast <note>the Manticore, a legendary creature</note>, which had a human head but the rest of its body was that of a lion (much like the Sphinx, the Chimera, and things similar to these).

## VI. The College of the Gymnosophists in Ethiopia.

The Gymnosophists <term>Gymnosophists</term> <note>original: *Gymnosophistæ*; literally "naked philosophers," a group of ancient ascetics known for their pursuit of wisdom and their rejection of clothing and worldly comforts</note> lived in Ethiopia, beyond the region of Memnon <note>Memnon was a mythical Ethiopian king and son of Eos (the Dawn) who fought in the Trojan War</note>, on a high hill. Thespion, the eldest among them, presided over them while sitting beneath an elm tree. This tree greeted Apollonius <note>Apollonius of Tyana, a 1st-century CE philosopher and miracle-worker whose travels were recorded by Philostratus</note> by his own name in a thin and feminine, yet articulate, voice, and even bowed itself toward him. Apollonius debated with them concerning the nature of things, sacred rites, magic, and other secrets for which he himself was most eager. Nilus, a man of gravity and prudence, followed Apollonius after leaving the College of the Gymnosophists. Caelius <note>likely Lodovico Ricchieri, also known as Caelius Rhodiginus, an Italian scholar</note> writes that Budda <note>the Buddha</note>, the leader of the Gymnosophists, brought forth a virgin from his side. Although this may be preternatural—much like the voice and the bowing of the tree—and might seem to have occurred by the power of a demon <note>original: *vi dęmonis*; in this context, it suggests a supernatural spirit or agency outside of the Christian tradition</note>, they nevertheless dealt with the works of nature in other matters. These works seemed no less marvelous to those ignorant of the causes of things than the events just described. The Gymnosophists are said to have hated mental sluggishness and idleness; they lived naked in the forests from sunrise to sunset

They remained staring at the Sun with unmoving eyes. On the burning sands, too, they stood on alternating feet throughout the day. Among them, all the youths gathered from various places and duties for their meal. The masters would ask each one what fruitful work they had accomplished from sunrise until that time of day. Those who had nothing to report were driven back out to work without having eaten.

## VII. The College of the Pythagoreans in Magna Graecia, or Southern Italy.

Pythagoras learned the secrets of nature and magic, along with <term>Metempsychosis</term> <note>original: *Metapsychosi*; the transmigration of the soul into a new body after death</note>, from the priests in Egypt and from the Chaldeans in Babylon. His disciples pooled all their resources together so that everything was held in common. They observed a period of silence for five whole years before they were admitted into the college and into the presence of the teacher. Many laws of this society are recorded; if anyone committed themselves to these, they were immediately accepted as associates and friends. These laws are called the symbols, or the sayings, of Pythagoras; besides which there exist

## IX. *The College of the Moors at Fez.*

# CHAPTER SIX.

*From which it may not seem absurd to believe that some such society exists even now, especially since it has revealed itself through its writings and deeds.*

Hitherto, it is sufficiently clear from the circumstances and the consensus of all that the **Colleges** <note>original: *Collegia*; here referring to the organized groups or secret societies mentioned in previous chapters</note> enumerated were not merely established for the teaching of common doctrines, which are usually taught elsewhere in schools, but rather for the purpose of imparting, under the strictest pledge of silence, the most secret mysteries of nature—mysteries which it was not appropriate for everyone, whether of high or low station, to know without distinction. For not only do all these **colonies** <note>original: *coloniae*; used here to describe the various branches or offshoots of these philosophical societies spread across different lands</note> seem to depend upon a single author, namely **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure from late antiquity considered the father of alchemy, magic, and Egyptian philosophy</note>, and this knowledge of secrets seems to have progressed by being handed down, as if from hand to hand, from one nation to another, but they also agree in their laws and rules, as well as the patterns of life and conduct prescribed for them, according to the requirements of their time and religion. Their **axioms** <note>fundamental principles or established rules of the society</note> are that they must honor and fear God above all things, and that of men...

Even though the **right of primogeniture** <term>primogeniture: the legal or customary right of the firstborn child to inherit the family's entire estate or authority</term> holds weight, it must be admitted that more was granted to the early fathers than to those of us reserved for the world’s "old age"—unless we remedy this deficiency through the **literary records** <note>original: *literarum monumenta*; referring to the written heritage, books, and historical documents of the past</note> and teachings we have received from them.

This is done to such an extent that what was granted to the ancients by a special privilege of nature is compensated in modern people by **experience** <note>original: *experientia*; often implying experimental knowledge or empirical observation</note> and the observation of many examples in the various arts. 

Therefore, if it is possible for our age to be capable of understanding those things which **antiquity** <note>the ancient world, particularly the classical and biblical eras</note> recognized, who but a fool would contradict those who have sufficiently revealed themselves through their deeds and writings? They have demonstrated, not without reason, that they are individuals of such great stature and importance. 

But if it were permissible to deny everything we have not seen ourselves—whether known through constant report, the accounts of trustworthy men, or from the books and records of the ancients which agree with one another, as well as with nature and **reason** <note>original: *ratione*; referring to the logical order of the universe</note>...

Furthermore, there is a natural desire in man to know anything. Indeed, it is held that there is no desire for what is unknown, and for that reason, one should not wish for things to remain hidden simply so they are not sought after. For how can things be acquired before they are desired? How can they be desired before they are heard of? For from this, if not a single utility <note>original: *non una utilitas*; likely completing the thought from the previous page's discussion on the "utility" of the order</note>, at least the **glory of God** could be increased, by looking not toward action, but toward the **contemplation** of divine gifts. For if the work itself is observed in very few, yet the speculation of the same is observed in many, it seems necessary.

Furthermore, the author and founder of the **Order** <note>original: *Ordinis*</note> itself willed that this **owl** <term>noctua: a bird of wisdom that typically remains hidden in the dark; here used as a metaphor for the secret society</term> should not always live in the shadows, but after a certain number of years should fly out even at midday, although the cohorts of other birds may attack and harass it from ambush, since it has the **Phoenix** <note>A mythical bird representing rebirth and unique excellence</note> as the king of birds and the **Hawk** as its protector. He undoubtedly had probable causes for this, some of which the ***Fama*** and ***Confessio*** <note>Refers to the *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614) and *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the foundational manifestos of the Rosicrucian Order</note> demonstrate, and which therefore need not be brought forward by us. Indeed, it is reckoned that there is no desire for the unknown, and therefore it is not to be wished that things remain hidden, lest they never be sought after. For how can they be acquired before they are desired? How can they be desired before they are heard of? In the very ancient tomb of **Belus** <note>Belus was a legendary king of Babylon, often associated with the god Marduk</note>, **Xerxes** <note>Xerxes I, the Persian King known for his invasion of Greece</note> found a glass urn where the corpse of the dead man lay in oil. The urn was indeed not full, but a certain empty space remained above the body. Lying next to the urn was a small column on which it was read as carved: that whoever opened the tomb and did not refill the urn would afterwards fare very badly and pay the penalties of impiety, because he had provoked the spirits <note>original: *Manes*; the deified souls of the deceased in Roman tradition</note> of the deceased by this sacrilege. 

**Xerxes**, trembling at this, ordered oil to be poured in, but nevertheless, the urn did not fill. Again and again he ordered it to be poured in more copiously, but it added no increase to the level. When he saw that he was wasting his effort, he closed the monument and departed sadly. Not long after, his army having been defeated in Greece, he was killed by his own son.

# CHAPTER EIGHT.

*If the German society was to be revealed,*
*it could not or should not have been*
*manifested otherwise than was done*
*by those two writings, the Fama and*
*the Confessio.*

Since, therefore, no reason remains why the aforementioned philosophical society <note>Original: *societas philosophica*; here referring to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood</note> should hide any longer—and since the last will of its founder <note>Christian Rosenkreuz, the legendary founder whose tomb was said to have been discovered after 120 years</note> impels it toward revelation (together with the logic and the benefit for rational men to be derived therefrom, especially in this time when the final act or catastrophe <note>Original: *catastrophe*; Maier uses the theatrical term for the resolution of a play to suggest the impending end of the world or a great historical shift</note> of the world’s stage is believed to be imminent, and a transformation of all things is daily expected)—how else could this have been brought to effect than through those writings we have observed? For it seems reasonable that the *Fama* <note>The *Fama Fraternitatis* (1614), the first Rosicrucian manifesto, which announced the existence of the order to the world</note> should precede the *Confessio* <note>The *Confessio Fraternitatis* (1615), the second manifesto, which provided more specific doctrinal details</note>. The former appears to call from a distance, while the latter seeks one from nearby; thus, it is the correct order to proceed from the universal to the particular, and not the other way around:

Who is not believing of the former <note>Referring to the *Fama Fraternitatis* mentioned on the preceding page</note> will be much less so of this latter one <note>The *Confessio Fraternitatis*</note>. For it did not seem advisable to pour everything out all at once, since a matter is judged "quickly enough, if it is judged well enough" <note>Maier cites the Latin proverb *sat cito, si sat bene*, attributed to Cato the Elder, suggesting that quality and careful consideration are more important than speed</note>. The path must be tested at the shallow fords, lest danger arises from rashness or loss from haste.

Whenever Apelles <note>The most celebrated painter of ancient Greece (4th century BCE), known for his technical perfection</note> had completed a painting with singular artistry, he was accustomed to display it at the crossroads <note>Original: *triviis*; literally where three roads meet, a place of public gathering</note> for the judgment of the common people. He would hide himself behind the work in a more secret spot so that he might take note of what true or false things were said, and to hear if perhaps some fault he had not noticed might be revealed by the criticism of others. 

It is believable that the so-called Venerable Society <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood</note> acted in this same manner with these books of theirs. They put them forth to be examined and weighed by the learned and the rational—not by those preoccupied with the pursuit of profit and wealth, nor the greedy and the unlearned. For they did not send out ravens, but doves to test the way—that is, for the purpose of testing and assessing the human mind—to see if they might bring back branches of green olive <note>An allusion to the story of Noah’s Ark in Genesis 8; the raven represents those who do not return to the spiritual fold, while the dove brings a sign of peace and new life</note>, which are signs of good will. This is the same duty that is imagined to be performed for the pagan gods by Mercury or Hermes (who is considered the "interpreter" <term>Interpres</term> by the Greeks), so that he might clearly explain their divine mind to men,

The same is believed to have been intended by the **German Society of the R.C.** <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis)</note> regarding their books—namely, that they should run as intermediaries, acting as interpreters, to signify their intentions to others. These are human means, which they have used as men, *thinking nothing human to be foreign to them* <note>A famous Latin adage from the playwright Terence: "Homo sum, humani nihil a me alienum puto." It suggests a spirit of empathy and shared human experience.</note>. 

If the divine gift of **Printing** <term>Typography; the "divine art" of the printing press</term> had been applied to the publishing of useful or necessary writings—especially in this age, where wits scatter their excess (not to say their insolence and cynical eloquence <note>Original: *caninum eloquium*, literally "dog-like eloquence," referring to biting or snarling criticism associated with the Cynic philosophers</note>), nay, even their vices and blasphemies, upon every wall and corner of the world—as much as it is used in these commentaries, they would then look more toward the praise of God and the benefit of their neighbor, for whom it is intended. 

For what is more to be desired by one devoted to **Wisdom** than to hear the reasons and circumstances of some **Philosophical College** <note>A reference to the proposed Rosicrucian "College" or invisible gathering of scholars</note>? A college occupied not only with the knowledge of natural things, but also with their practical application; one that provides healing to the poor for free, distributes alms, and makes certain lovers of philosophy partakers of such a great secret. Their gathering would be a **Practical School**, which matches words with deeds and deeds with words, and penetrates the inner sanctuaries of Nature, she herself not being unwilling.

# CHAPTER NINE.
---

*That nothing is contained in these books that is contrary to reason, nature, experience, or the possibility of the matter.*

Since the secrets of such arts are scattered throughout the books of the said fraternity <note>referring to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood</note> and are brought forward as examples, it seems worth the effort to inquire whether anything is contained in them that is contrary to reason, nature, experience, good morals, or the possibility of the thing itself. For whatever conflicts with these principles allows for some objection, but whatever agrees with them allows none. Let **Reason** first conduct an inspection, then the remaining criteria in order: They claim that they are able to know all "knowables" <note>original: *Scibilia*; a Scholastic term for things that are capable of being known through human intellect.</note>, even those things which occur in remote places, as far as the **Ganges** <note>A river in India, used here as a literary shorthand for the farthest reaches of the known world.</note>. These things are surely to be wondered at by others rather than imitated. But they explain themselves: namely, that this is achieved through the visitation of various places, the investigation of new things, the reading of published books, and the comparison of events with their causes, just as from **physiognomy** <term>physiognomy: the pseudo-scientific practice of assessing a person's character or predicting their future based on their outer appearance, especially the face.</term>

<meta>catchword: mun-</meta>

<summary>In this chapter, the author begins a systematic defense of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood's claims, arguing that their seemingly miraculous knowledge of distant events is based on empirical methods like travel and study rather than the supernatural.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucianism, Reason, Nature, Experience, Scibilia, Ganges, Physiognomy, Epistemology</keywords>

of the world, to which observations of the constellations are added, they foreknow many things of the future, remember things past, observe things present, and see those who are absent as if they were before their eyes. What they report regarding the secrets of natural things and those things obtainable by art <note>original: *arte parabilium*; in this context, "art" refers to alchemy, chemistry, and human ingenuity that assists nature.</note> does not declare war on reason, even though it may seem contradictory to the unrefined and the ignorant. Concerning the **Universal Medicine** <term>Universal Medicine: a legendary "panacea" or alchemical elixir believed to cure all diseases and prolong life, as well as "cure" or transmute base metals into gold.</term>, both for metals and for men, as well as the particular remedies that heal certain diseases through the properties of nature—the things they promise are proved by reason and are demonstrated elsewhere by the force of their causes. These particular remedies are recognized even by the **Galenists** <term>Galenists: followers of the traditional medical system of Galen, focused on balancing the four humors.</term>, nor do the **Methodists** or the **Empiricists** <term>Empiricists: a medical sect that relied on practical observation and trial-and-error rather than overarching theories.</term> reject them; instead, all embrace and use them unanimously. of the world, to which observations of the constellations are added, they foreknow many things of the future, remember things of the past, observe the present, and see those who are absent as if they were before them. Let them, therefore, first practice their military service <note>A metaphor for rigorous intellectual training or discipline.</note> under the leadership of **Reason**, and afterward they will notice nothing adverse to themselves in these matters.

Concerning the **Universal Medicine** <term>Universal Medicine: a legendary substance, often identified with the Philosopher's Stone, believed to cure all diseases and perfect "imperfect" metals like lead into gold.</term>—as much for metals as for men—as well as the particular remedies that heal certain diseases by the property of nature, what they promise is approved by reason and demonstrated elsewhere by the weight of causes brought forward. These particulars are even acknowledged by the **Galenists** <term>Galenists: followers of the medical system of Galen (2nd century AD), which dominated European medicine for centuries by focusing on the balance of the four humors.</term>, nor do the **Methodists** <term>Methodists: a medical sect from antiquity that emphasized general "communities" of disease rather than individual symptoms.</term> or the **Empirics** <term>Empirics: practitioners who relied on practical experience and observation rather than established medical theories.</term> turn away from them, but all unanimously embrace and make use of them. Some work from the signatures of characteristics <note>original: *signaturis characterismis*; a reference to the "Doctrine of Signatures," the belief that plants or minerals resembling body parts were intended by God to heal those parts.</note>, others from the sympathy of the stars and the conspira- <note>The word continues on the next page as "conspiratione," meaning harmony or "breathing together."</note>

Although they are not perceived equally by everyone, as natural things are, yet this does not prevent them from being so, seeing as they are considered **secrets** <note>original: *arcana*</note> which ought to be unknown to the common folk.

...already in this part of Europe, who, although they have arrived after long journeys, are nevertheless not all accepted. Instead, a selection is established from the entire gathering. The reason why a soldier is not always chosen for war, or why every sick person is not cured by a doctor, does not always lie with the individual; rather, it lies in the circumstances and accidents of affairs. So too, perhaps, no one among those called gave cause for their own rejection, but rather it was the nature and condition of the matter itself. For as it is often said of a **banquet**, <note>original: *conuiuio*</note> it ought to begin with the number of the **Graces** <note>original: *Gratiarum*; the three goddesses of charm and grace. Since it is proper that all harm be prevented by them—even that of private individuals, and much more so that of the world, that is, of the men in the world...

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical defense of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood’s secrecy, moving from the personal discipline of silence (as seen with Agatho and Demosthenes on the previous page) to a broader argument about the corruption of "good" things when they are exposed to the "impure" general public.</meta>

Harm would follow, as has already been stated, if the **Secrets** of the **Rosicrucian Brotherhood** were made known. If these secrets are good, [their exposure brings harm through misuse]; if they are bad, they would certainly be damaging, yet even then they should not be revealed but kept to their authors. 

In this way, **Clement the Theologian** <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD), a Christian philosopher who often wrote about the "mysteries" of the faith.</note> and the philosopher **Demonax** (who lived in the time of the Emperor Hadrian) argued concerning the hidden **Eleusinian mysteries**: <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece, centered on the goddess Demeter.</note> "*For,*" they said, "*if these rites did not contain something shameful,* <note>original: *meretricia*; literally "pertaining to harlots," implying something base or indecent.</note> *why would they not be made manifest?*" 

A counter-argument is offered to them: it is precisely because they do *not* contain shameful or common deeds that they are kept hidden. For this reason, the immoral, the adulterous, the impure, and the profane are kept away, while the chaste, pious, and wise are attracted and initiated into them. 

It is well known that the best things, in their proper use, become the very worst when abused. The sweetest **honey** turns into the most bitter **gall**. **Pericles**, <note>Historical note: The author likely confuses Pericles here with other Athenian leaders like Themistocles or Alcibiades, who famously fled to the Persian King after being exiled. Pericles lived and died in Athens.</note> once the most valiant leader of the Athenians in war, became the greatest threat to his own fatherland after being driven into exile to the court of the Persian King. 

In the same way, although the secrets of this society are of the highest and best sort, there is no doubt that if they were opened to the common people, they would be corrupted by the nature of the masses, which is the worst of all. **Wines** and fragrant **preserves** take on the foul taste and smell of the impure vessels in which they are stored for too long. **Herophilus** <note>An ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Anatomy."</note> said that *medicines are the hands of the Gods...* <note>original: *medicamenta & manus Deorum*; the phrase is completed by the catchword "rum" at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>This page justifies the Rosicrucian policy of secrecy by arguing that high truths, like the Eleusinian mysteries, are hidden not because they are shameful, but to protect them from being corrupted by the "impure" nature of the common people.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucian secrets, Eleusinian mysteries, Clement of Alexandria, Demonax, secrecy vs. disclosure, corruption of knowledge, Pericles, Herophilus, elitism</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the extreme secrecy of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood, transitioning from the silence of Demosthenes and the Desert Fathers to the necessity of protecting sacred "secrets" (Arcana) from the corruption of the unrefined public.</meta>

...harm, as has already been said, would be connected to it if the **Secrets of the R.C. Brotherhood** <note>original: *Arcana Fraternitatis R.C.*; referring to the Rosicrucians.</note> were made known, if they are good; if indeed they are bad, then they would be harmful, but in any case they should not be revealed and should be reserved for their authors. Thus **Clement the Theologian** <note>Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD), an early Christian teacher who often compared Christian "mysteries" to Greek ones.</note> and **Demonax** the philosopher <note>A celebrated 2nd-century Cynic philosopher known for his wit.</note> in the time of **Hadrian**, argue concerning the hidden **Eleusinian mysteries**: <note>The most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece, dedicated to the goddesses Demeter and Persephone.</note> *For, they say, unless they contained the works of harlots, why would they not be shown?* 

The answer to them is reciprocal: because they do *not* contain the works of harlots or common things, they are therefore hidden; and for that reason harlots, adulterers, the impure, and the profane are kept away, while the chaste, pious, and prudent are attracted and initiated into them. 

Moreover, it is not unknown that the best things, in their true use, become the worst in their abuse. The sweetest honey turns into the bitterest gall. **Pericles**, <note>The author likely refers to Themistocles or perhaps Alcibiades, as Pericles was famously loyal to Athens. However, the manuscript specifically names Pericles as the leader who fled to the Persian King.</note> the bravest leader of the Athenians in war, was (after being driven into exile) most hostile to his own fatherland while staying with the King of the Persians. 

Thus, even if the secrets of this society are the highest and best, there is no doubt that if they were opened to the common crowd, they would take on the nature of the crowd, which is the worst. Wines and fragrant confections taste and smell of the impure vessels in which they are held for a long time. **Herophilus** <note>An ancient Greek physician (335–280 BC) who was one of the first to perform scientific dissections.</note> called *medicines the hands of the Gods* <note>The sentence continues on the next page; the catchword "rum" completes the Latin *Deorum* (of the Gods).</note>

<summary>The author justifies Rosicrucian secrecy by arguing that sacred knowledge is like a delicate medicine or fine wine—it is easily corrupted or turned "bitter" if handled by the unworthy or the common public.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucian secrecy, Eleusinian mysteries, Clement of Alexandria, Demonax, corruption of knowledge, Pericles, Herophilus, sacred medicine</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page concludes a philosophical discussion on the neutrality of power and objects, arguing that secrecy is necessary because most people lack the character to use powerful "secrets" correctly. It transitions into Chapter 11, which examines historical and mythological archetypes of silence.</meta>

...[and] affirmed nothing, according to the reasoning of the user and the one applying them in the proper place and time. Fire and the sword—to whom do they not cause harm, and to whom are they not of benefit? Use and abuse establish the distinction between utility and injury. What is better than **gold**? Indeed, what is worse or more harmful? What is more significant than the human tongue itself, which that **Phrygian Storyteller** <note>Aesop (c. 620–564 BCE), the legendary Greek fabulist who was said to be a Phrygian slave. He famously described the tongue as both the best and worst part of a man because it can offer both wisdom and slander.</note> recognized as both the best and worst limb? Indeed, what is better than a good woman or man, or worse than a bad one? For "man is to man both a god and a demon," <note>original: *homo homini Deus & dæmon*; a variation of the common proverb "man is a wolf to man," here emphasizing that human beings have the capacity for both divine benevolence and diabolical cruelty.</note> as the saying goes. However, the other reasons why these **secrets** <term>arcana: hidden or mysterious knowledge, specifically referring to the internal teachings of the Rosicrucians</term> should not be revealed have been sufficiently handed down elsewhere by others and by ourselves; we wish to refer the reader to those works, while here we shall produce more extensive examples of silence.

# CHAPTER ELEVEN.

*The Egyptian deities, **Sigalion** or **Harpocrates**, were placed upon altars together with **Sphinxes**, which denoted silence, because*

<sig>E</sig>
<meta>catchword: and Py-</meta>

What is more significant than the **human tongue** itself, which that **Phrygian Storyteller** <note>original: *Phrygius ille Fabulator*; referring to Aesop, the legendary Greek fabulist said to be from Phrygia, who famously described the tongue as both the best and worst part of a person.</note> recognized as the best and the worst member? <meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient deities and philosophical schools that mandated silence and secrecy. It completes the mention of Sigalion (begun on the previous page as "wit...") and moves into Egyptian symbolism and the Pythagorean school.</meta>

**Sigalion** <note>A Roman name for the Egyptian god Harpocrates, specifically in his role as the god of silence.</note> or **Harpocrates** was worshipped, or rather his image was placed upon altars; this figure held its left hand to cover its private parts, while the index and middle fingers of the right hand were pressed against its lips. This was done to indicate that the sacred rites performed there possessed a hidden meaning and were to be suppressed in silence. 

For the same reason, **Sphinxes**—with the face of a maiden, a feathered body like a bird's, and the feet of a lion—were seen everywhere, and the gathered people were commanded to "favor with their tongues" <note>original: *linguis favere*, a ritual command to maintain a holy silence so as not to pronounce any ill-omened words.</note>. This was because their secret matters were more abstruse than could be investigated, and more sublime than could be reached by human wit. 

The **Hieroglyphic** writings of the **Egyptians** and their poetic allegories served this same purpose. By these means, as if with shadows, they veiled the rays of truth—much like vine-leaves cover the growing clusters of grapes—lest they be harmed by the injuries of wind or heat; that is to say, by the **common people**.

-ing and reputation; and so great was the steadfastness of the disciples, both of which became famous because of their knowledge of secrets. **Amyclae** was destroyed by silence <note>A reference to a legendary Greek or Italian city whose leaders, tired of false alarms, forbade anyone to mention the enemy's approach; when the enemy actually arrived, no one dared speak, and the city fell.</note>, but that was a misguided kind; otherwise, silence has harmed no one, whereas speaking has harmed very many.

A person is seen to have been given by nature two eyes, two ears, and two nostrils, but only a single **tongue**, and that one is well-fortified within the wall of the teeth, lest it should blurt out anything against the bit, or more than is fitting. For just as it is difficult to use an untamed pony at one's command, so it is with the tongue.

Hence the philosopher **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Citium, the founder of Stoicism, known for his personal restraint and the teaching that we have two ears and one mouth so we may hear more and speak less.</note>, when he had invited the ambassadors of King **Antigonus** (sent to Athens) to dinner along with several other philosophers, and while all the others were showing off their learning with a multitude of words, he alone remained silent. When the ambassadors asked the reason for his silence, he replied: *The most difficult of all things is to keep a check on one’s speech.* By this saying, he not only rebuked those talkative pseudo-philosophers, but also excused himself for his own prolonged silence. Concerning **Tantalus**, who revealed the secrets of the Gods <note>In Greek mythology, Tantalus was punished in the underworld for revealing divine secrets; he was forced to stand in a pool of water beneath fruit trees, both of which receded whenever he reached for them.</note> to men (which in truth were nothing other than these secrets of nature, as we have demonstrated elsewhere), **Ovid** sings thus:

->*Tantalus’s talkative tongue brought this upon him.*<- <note>original: "Quærit aquas in aquis & poma fugacia captat / Tantalus hoc illi garrula lingua dedit."</note>

# CHAPTER FOURTEEN.

### *A response to the adversaries of Chemistry*
### *directed under this name.*

In these opinions, there are others very similar—men not unlearned in literature, but either envious or, in this matter under discussion, inexperienced; their judgment appears equal and parallel to those already mentioned. Moreover, they have not seemed satisfied with themselves unless they also stir up others as followers of their opinion and trumpet forth their own judgment concerning that society <note>The "society" likely refers to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis), whose early 17th-century manifestos sparked a massive literary war between defenders and detractors of their "chemical" and spiritual secrets.</note>. But how will this **worldly phantom** <term>phantasma mundanum: The author suggests the critics view alchemy as a mere illusion or ghost of the physical world rather than a reality.</term> or **deceptive mask** <term>larua lusoria: A mask used by actors or in play; a metaphor for something that appears to be one thing but is actually another.</term> be proven to those who, besides their own ignorance of how great a thing this could be, have nothing to claim? They deny the **Chemical Art** <term>Artem Chymicam: In this period, "Chemistry" and "Alchemy" were not yet distinct; the term encompasses the laboratory pursuit of transmuting metals and creating universal medicines.</term>, and therefore, whatever they think has proceeded from this as a source, they not undeservedly remove from the nature of things, lest by denying the cause they concede the effect. But it would have been better not to have stirred up this **noxious vapor** <term>mephitin: Historically, a foul-smelling or poisonous exhalation from the earth; here used metaphorically for a stinking controversy.</term> or provoked **hornets** <term>crabrones: A classical metaphor for stirring up trouble or provoking sharp-tongued opponents.</term> of this kind, and rather to have blamed their own inability to grasp such great matters.

<meta>catchword: Because</meta>

<summary>The author critiques "learned" but inexperienced opponents of alchemy who dismiss the art out of hand to avoid admitting its results. He suggests their opposition stems from a personal inability to understand the subject rather than any factual basis.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, chemistry, Rosicrucians, academic envy, chemical art, polemic, mephitis</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the "Chemical Art" and the associated "Society" (the Rosicrucians) against skeptical critics. Following the previous page's warning about "noxious" opinions, the author here argues that the reality of natural science is independent of human disbelief, comparing alchemical truths to historical and mythological claims.</meta>

But if the **Chemistry** <term>Chymia: At this time, alchemy and chemistry were treated as one science of matter and its transformations, often involving the search for the philosopher's stone or universal medicines.</term> that they deny is indeed true, why shouldn't this also be true—this science and profession of natural things that depends upon it, as well as the **society** <note>A reference to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis), whose existence and alchemical secrets were the subject of intense public debate in the early 17th century.</note> which practices and expresses it through actual labor? We have already briefly demonstrated here, and more extensively elsewhere, that this is true, using both logical reasoning and the **Experience** <note>Latin: *Experientia*. In this context, it refers to empirical evidence or experimental results rather than just internal feeling.</note> of men who are beyond all reproach. Therefore, they have written nothing against it that is worthy of belief, nor have they refuted anything in a way that proves it cannot exist. Just because something seems **incredible** to them, does that make it **impossible**?

The works which the aforementioned brothers profess are natural things, assisted by **Art** <term>Ars: Often used to refer specifically to the "Alchemical Art," where human skill completes or accelerates what nature started.</term> and brought into actual realization. If they claimed such things, then perhaps these critics would find some excuse for their disbelief. And yet, while **Pagan poets** <note>Latin: *Poetae Ethnici*. Referring to classical Greek and Roman authors like Ovid, who wrote of such metamorphoses.</note> accept those myths as true (even if they are false), we recognize these chemical truths as being most certain. Reality does not change just because the notions or concepts of the mind change; nor do **Beings** <term>Entia: A philosophical term for things that have actual existence in reality.</term> increase just because our thoughts about them multiply. **Alexander the Great** <note>The Macedonian king famously wept because he believed there were no more worlds to conquer; here, the author uses him to illustrate that seeking does not always lead to immediate finding.</note>, seeking new worlds to conquer within the **Ocean** <note>In antiquity, the *Oceanus* was the great river believed to encircle the entire world.</note> and not 

<summary>The author argues that the reality of alchemy and the Rosicrucian society is based on nature and experience rather than myth, asserting that a critic's disbelief does not negate physical truth. He contrasts the "natural works" of alchemy with the impossible fables of ancient poets to show that chemical science is grounded in reality.</summary>

But now we will let the horse into the open field—that is, we shall deal with those who assume they possess some great worth in the arts and sciences. And indeed, we concede that these learned men excel—and not slightly—in authority and practical experience, so that they seem to have served (and not without merit) in the **Camps of Minerva** <note>original: "Minervæ Castris." Minerva was the Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare; to serve in her camp means to be a dedicated scholar.</note> and perhaps even in the **Workshops of Vulcan** <note>original: "Vulcani officinis." Vulcan was the god of fire and smithing, a common metaphor in this period for the laboratories of alchemists and experimental philosophers.</note>, being gifted with titles and names grand enough. 

But as for their denial that the **College of the Rosicrucian Fraternity** <term>Collegium Fraternitatis R. C.: The legendary Brotherhood of the Rosy Cross, a secretive group of sages claiming deep spiritual and scientific knowledge.</term> exists among things that can be known, they are understood to be flattering themselves in this matter rather than truly opposing the Brotherhood. For they attribute to themselves the knowledge <note>original: "scientiam"</note> of all nature and the power of every art, and in both, they judge themselves by their own standard. How, they ask, could such great **philosophers** <note>original: "philosophi"; here meaning seekers of universal truth or scientists.</note> exist anywhere, whom we i- <note>The text breaks here to be completed as "ignore" on the following page.</note>

...ignore? What experiments of art might they know that are hidden from us? What proofs of natural science <note>original: "naturalis scientiæ"</note> might they provide that have not been explored by us to the finest detail? <note>original: "ad vnguem" — literally "to the fingernail," a Roman expression for something finished with perfect precision.</note> But indeed, we do not perceive any reason why they could have been, or could exist; we cannot imagine a place where they might hide; we encounter their metallic <note>Refers to alchemical works involving the transmutation of metals.</note> or medicinal works nowhere. They will not persuade the more sharp-sighted or circumspect with this argument, but only the ignorant and unlearned. These are their reasons—more fortified and firm than any castle, or so they think—which no one is able to contradict unless he dares to set his own battering rams and catapults against their "universal knowledge" <note>original: "scientiæ vniuersali"</note>. Should we take so much upon ourselves, or should these **Suffeni** <term>Suffeni: A reference to Suffenus, a character from the Roman poet Catullus used to describe a person who is conceited and puffed up with his own mediocre talents.</term> be left behind? 

I believe we should offer sacrifices to the Truth rather than to the **Self-Love** <term>Philautia: A Greek term for excessive self-love or conceit, often personified in Renaissance literature as an enemy of true learning.</term> of others. Even the most learned have not refused to confess that they become more learned by growing old, seeking to learn something more even if (as they have testified) they had one foot in **Charon’s Boat** <term>Cymba Charontis: The mythological ferry that carries the souls of the dead across the river Styx to the underworld; the phrase implies being on the brink of death.</term>. But perhaps they will say that these professors propose absurdities which agree with neither reason nor experience; things which I have neither read nor known. I freely admit that even in things most clearly stated, a sense of obscurity can be seized upon and certain doubts can be raised. <meta>This page continues the defense of the "secrets" mentioned on page 104. The previous page ended by asking why critics do not permit these secrets to be a "standard of truth," which is how this page begins.</meta>

The matter has already been proven possible by reason and by the example of other **Colleges** <note>Likely referring to other learned societies or academic bodies of the era.</note>, and no one, unless they are entirely unrefined, will deny it.

The circumstances that define this matter are indeed manifold; namely, how the first author of the society <note>A reference to "C.R.C." or Christian Rosenkreuz, the legendary founder of the Rosicrucian Order.</note> acquired the art from the **Arabs**, being recognized also by his name and family, and from there traveled to the **Moors of Fez** <note>Fez, in modern-day Morocco, was a famous medieval center for the study of alchemy, mathematics, and philosophy.</note>. It is held for certain that this craftsmanship was once transferred from the **Egyptians** to the **Arabs**. For this reason, the columns of **Osiris and Isis** <note>The principal deities of the Egyptian pantheon, often associated by alchemists with the masculine and feminine principles of nature.</note>, which are entirely **alchemical** <note>original: "Chymicæ" — in the 17th century, this term encompassed both chemistry and alchemy.</note> in nature, are believed to have been erected in **Nysa** in Arabia, where **Dionysus** <note>The Greek god of wine and transformation, frequently linked to ancient mystery traditions.</note> was also born; and it is said that the **Phoenix** migrated from Arabia into Egypt once its cycle of years was completed.

From the Arabs to the Moors, all the arts—such as **philosophy**, **Mathematics** <term>Mathesis: The study of the mathematical sciences, often including their mystical or astrological applications.</term>, **Physics**, and **Medicine**, along with the **occult science of natural things**—passed over, among whom there were very many Philosophers and Physicians, and not...

<meta>This page continues the historical and philosophical defense of the Rosicrucian "College." It picks up from the previous page's discussion of how the society’s founder (Christian Rosenkreuz) acquired secret knowledge from the Arabs and sought to reform European sciences.</meta>

...and not unskillful **chemists** <note>original: "Chymici" – in the 17th century, this term referred to both alchemists and chemists, who were then part of the same discipline.</note> then flourished, such as **Averrhoes** <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), a famous Andalusian philosopher and polymath.</note>, **Gilgil** <note>Likely a reference to an Arabic authority, possibly Al-Bitruji or Al-Jildaki, frequently cited in medieval alchemical texts.</note>, **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), the Persian "father of modern medicine."</note>, **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), the legendary father of Arabic chemistry.</note>, and countless others. From these, the craft of **chemistry** was propagated to other most learned men of **Europe**, namely **Albertus Magnus**, **Arnold** <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a physician and alchemist.</note>, **Lully** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a major Catalan philosopher and mystic.</note>, and those like them. Based on a consideration of the years, it appears that the first author of this **College** <note>A reference to Christian Rosenkreuz, the founder of the Rosicrucian Order.</note> lived during their time, or a little after. 

Thus, the attainment of the **chemical** craft appears not to have been entirely unusual in that age, but rather quite frequent, as is evident from many contemporaries of that century. And although the **Moors** were first driven out of **Spain** by **Ferdinand the Just** <note>Ferdinand I of Aragon (1380–1416), known as "the Just."</note> a little over a hundred years ago, nevertheless the kingdoms of **Castile** and certain others remained untouched by the **Moors**, and **Fez** <note>original: "Fessanum" — a city in Morocco then famous as a center of occult and scientific learning.</note> in **Africa** flourished regardless.

One might ask why he <note>The founder of the Order.</note> sought a reformation of the arts? Namely, so that **medicine**, **physics**, **astronomy**, and other arts might agree with their own axioms <note>Fundamental principles or established truths.</note>—which ought to be such that both the hidden and the manifest virtues of things are reduced to them, and that principles correspond to their end. A similar judgment must be made concerning the effects: namely, that these should agree with their causes...

<summary>This page traces the transmission of alchemical and scientific knowledge from Arabic scholars to European masters, placing the Rosicrucian founder within this historical lineage. It argues that the "reformation of the arts" was necessary to align medicine, physics, and astronomy with their true hidden principles.</summary>

...did <term>chemical artists</term> <note>original: "artifices Chymici" — practitioners of alchemy and early chemistry.</note> not then flourish, such as **Averroes**, **Gilgil** <note>Likely referring to the Arabic astronomer Al-Bitruji (Alpetragius), whose name was often Latinized and cited in alchemical texts.</note>, **Avicenna**, **Geber**, and countless others? By considering the timeline, it appears that the first author of this **College** <note>The "College" refers to the Rosicrucian Brotherhood; the "author" is their legendary founder, Christian Rosenkreuz.</note> lived during their time, or shortly thereafter.

From this, the attainment of the chemical art seems not at all unusual for that age, but rather quite frequent, as is evident from many contemporaries of that century. These axioms ought to be such that both the **hidden** and the **manifest** virtues of things are traced back to them; and that the principles correspond to their intended purpose.

# CHAPTER SIXTEENTH

->*Others attribute everything to necromancy and superstition, to whom a response is made.*<-

There are, besides the critics already mentioned, others who—seeking to bolster the reputation of their own learning as well as their zeal for piety—attribute all the works of the aforementioned **Brotherhood** <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood, which in the early 17th century claimed to possess secret knowledge of nature, medicine, and alchemy.</note> to diabolical illusions; that is, to **necromancy** <note>original: "negromantiam" — while literally meaning "divination by the dead," in this period it was a catch-all term for "black magic" or any forbidden art involving demons.</note> and profane superstition. They believe this to be a more convenient path, as it excuses them from having to explain the causes of such magnificent natural phenomena. For those things which are diabolical surpass all of nature and possess no causes within nature, except in the will of the evil spirit, which is moved and incited by the most wicked of men. These people are **slanderers** <note>original: "Calūniiosi" (Calumniosi)</note> and blasphemers by their own choice, who, in order to cover their own ignorance, do not shrink from branding others with marks of shame. Truly, the causes of this— 

<meta>catchword: ce iu- [completing "huiusce iudicii" — of this very judgment]</meta>

<summary>This page begins a defense against critics who accuse the Rosicrucians of practicing black magic, arguing that such accusers use the label of "necromancy" simply to avoid admitting they do not understand the Brotherhood's advanced natural philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>necromancy, superstition, natural causes, slanders, Rosicrucian Brotherhood, diabolical illusions, ignorance</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 108. The last word of the previous page was "huius-" (of this), which is completed here by "ce iudicij" (judgment).</meta>

judgment of theirs, they seem to propose probable reasons: For, they say, those **brothers** profess to know what happens within and beyond the **Ganges** in **India**, and even those things which a person has in their mind, as is evident from the one who, at **Wetzlar** <note>A city in Germany, famously the seat of the Imperial Chamber Court; the author refers to a specific anecdote of Rosicrucian clairvoyance reported there.</note>, predicted to a host the very things he was about to say, and things that had happened in other places. Likewise, [they speak of] certain people being driven from a room by the burning of specific roots, the driving away of mice with a whip, straw that does not catch fire even when close to flames, a shot from a <term>musket</term> <note>original: "bombarda" — an early type of firearm or small cannon.</note> being directed with unerring aim, and other such feats. All these things, they say, are the works of <term>charlatans</term> <note>original: "circulatorum" — traveling entertainers or quacks who performed "miracles" for money.</note> and **sorcerers**, and not of men who are truly learned.

But wait, you rigid censor! Turn aside a little from that judgment of yours—which is more severe than that of the **Areopagus** <note>The high court of Athens, known for its stern and unyielding character.</note> or the bloody decree of **Draco** <note>An Athenian lawgiver whose legal code was so harsh it was said to be written in blood; hence the term "draconian."</note>—and against you (unless we are mistaken) exceptions most worthy of belief shall be raised. As far as can be seen from the books they have published, as well as the deeds of that <unclear>brother</unclear>, nothing is found in them that should rightly be attributed to **necromancy** or diabolical works. 

For regarding the first point—whereby they report that they know any occurring events—we respond that they know these things partly through the reports of others, and partly through travels to those places where such things [are known to happen]...

<summary>The author lists specific "miraculous" feats attributed to the Rosicrucians—such as clairvoyance, fire resistance, and trickery—which critics use as evidence of sorcery. He begins his defense by arguing these feats are based on natural knowledge and travel rather than the occult.</summary>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of the Rosicrucian "Brothers" against accusations of sorcery. The previous page ended by suggesting that their seemingly supernatural knowledge has natural explanations, beginning with the word "For..."</meta>

they can know these same things. For no place is now found inaccessible, even as far as the furthest **Indies** <note>original: "Indias" — referring generally to the East and West Indies, the frontiers of European exploration.</note>: the **Dutch** and the **English** now frequent most of the markets of the entire world, just as the **Portuguese** possess very large colonies in **India**; from these people, it can be obtained and known what is happening anywhere in those places. 

In addition to this, those instructed in a knowledge of **astronomy** that is not common can predict and divine many things, all of which occur naturally and through lawful means. Concerning things conceived in the mind and things done elsewhere—that they should know much of these—this also does not seem inconsistent with nature. We have known a certain **Spanish monk** who, in a large assembly, told many things—both past and future—to individuals based on <term>physiognomy</term> <note>original: "physiognomia" — the historical practice of assessing a person's character or destiny from their facial features.</note>, and who was therefore consulted by the common people so that he might predict each person's fortune. 

Perhaps each person’s own "character" is written upon their face, signifying either the good or bad fortune hanging over them; he who is able to read it will be able to divine no small amount from it. And wise men are otherwise accustomed to judge each person's character and disposition from their face, since the countenance often expresses a hidden emotion. **Momus** <note>The Greek god of mockery and blame. According to myth, he criticized the creator of man for not putting a window in the human chest so that thoughts could be seen.</note> is said to have criticized the construction of man because he did not have a window reaching to the heart,

<summary>The author argues that the Rosicrucians' supposedly magical knowledge is actually derived from modern global trade networks, advanced astronomy, and the study of physiognomy. --- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how internal thoughts and distant events can be known. The previous page mentioned Momus’s wish for a window in the human chest to see the heart; this page argues that the face serves as that very window.</meta>

through which his thoughts might be clearly seen;
But the face, insofar as it suffices,

no one is sorrowful whose right eye dances, or

concerning **Anger**, **Envy**, love, and other passions,

one's absence as if they were present is not within the power

whether good, like the **Prophets**, or evil,
like the **necromancers** <note>original: "negromantici" — practitioners of "black" divination involving the dead.</note>: It is well known that **Apollonius**, **Pythagoras**, the **Brahmins**, and the **Gymnosophists** <note>Ancient Indian philosophers known to the Greeks for their asceticism and "naked" wisdom.</note> knew these same things—though not by the same spirit: If the cause is to be judged by its effects, it was by none other than a good one: It is also clear from **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary source of Hermetic philosophy.</note> and countless others that a faculty of **prophesying** exists in many who are blessed by God with that supreme Gift, which **Petrus Bonus of Ferrara** <note>A 14th-century Italian physician and author of alchemical texts.</note> also suggests. Lest we insist too much on prediction from the stars—not that common sort, but the true kind resting on other principles: Although the remaining trifles are boasted of and sometimes performed by marketplace charlatans and **mountebanks** <note>original: "agyrtis" — traveling quacks or street performers claiming magical powers.</note>, it is nonetheless consistent with the truth that they have causes in nature, just like many other things which appear in **collections of notes** <note>original: "collectaneis" — common-place books where scholars gathered observations and secrets of nature.</note>.

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that many extraordinary phenomena are natural rather than demonic. The previous page discussed the human face as a "window" to the soul and touched upon the legitimacy of certain types of prophecy and hidden knowledge.</meta>

Such arts exist: **Cardano** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), an Italian polymath who wrote extensively on "subtlety" and the natural world.</note> in his books *On Subtlety* and *On the Variety of Things*, **Wecker** in his *Secrets*, **Porta** in *Natural Magic*, **Fallopio** in his own *Secrets*, and many others of this kind have collected wonders. To call these things "necromantic" <note>original: "negromantica" — a term for black magic or communication with the dead, which the author is defending against.</note> would be the act of madmen, since they are achieved through lawful, possible, and natural means—arising partly from manifest qualities and partly from <term>occult qualities</term> <note>Properties that are not immediately obvious to the senses, such as the pull of a magnet.</note>. 

What would these critics have said about the **magnet** and its works if the stone itself were unknown? What of that hanging urn of **Muhammad**? <note>A common medieval legend claimed the Prophet Muhammad's coffin was suspended in the air by powerful magnets.</note> What of the traveler on the sea, on land, and underground, guided by the power of the magnet? What of the marking of the hours of the day and a hundred other conveniences arising from the magnet? Would they not have asserted that all these were works of necromancy? 

What of the **dowsing rod** <note>original: "virgula diuinatoria"</note>, or the "wooden magnet" of metals, which indicates the presence of minerals by its own movement and leaping? What of **gunpowder** <note>original: "pyrio puluere"</note> and that horrific machine of war <note>Likely referring to the cannon or artillery.</note> which so truly mimics the lightning and thunder of **Salmoneus**? <note>In Greek mythology, Salmoneus was a king who was struck down by Zeus for using brass kettles and torches to fake the sound and light of divine thunder.</note> Concerning this, **Virgil** writes in the 6th book of the *Aeneid*:

> *I saw also Salmoneus paying a cruel penalty,*
> *While he imitated the flames of Jove and the sounds of Olympus.*

Would they not have judged the causes of this to be magical, or entirely divine, or even diabolical?

<summary>This page argues that many seemingly miraculous technologies, such as the compass, gunpowder, and dowsing rods, are actually natural phenomena based on "occult" or hidden properties of matter. The author warns against the ignorance of labeling advanced natural science as necromancy or demonic magic.</summary>

<keywords>magnetism, gunpowder, natural magic, Girolamo Cardano, Giambattista della Porta, dowsing rod, necromancy, Salmoneus, Virgil</keywords>

...not by mortals, but by gods, as they first believed? These are the same people who, in the beginning, thought that the letters <note>original: "Epistolas"</note> written by Spaniards to one another were vocal or "talkative," as if the papers were betraying what the people themselves had done. To wish to categorize these letters as magic <note>The author refers to the historical anecdote where Indigenous peoples in the Americas, unfamiliar with writing, believed letters were living entities or magical objects that could "talk" to the recipient.</note> would be to expose one's own brutal ignorance to the ridicule of others. Nevertheless, to those nations where the use of letters is unknown, such things can seem more marvelous and foreign to nature than the things I mentioned previously. There are a thousand ingenious devices which, although they are achieved through natural and entirely common means, are yet believed to oppose nature until they become understood. Therefore, let such premature judges of things cease to slander others whose works and effects they do not understand. Let them instead bring more mature judgments regarding natural science <note>original: "naturalium scientia"</note>; for although it possesses hidden <note>original: "arcanas"</note> causes unknown to the common people, it is nonetheless not to be attributed to illicit magic, but rather is to be

# CHAPTER
## SEVENTEEN.

*Many people, who deny neither the truth of Chemistry <note>original: "Chemiæ"; in this context, the author refers to the "Sacred Art" of alchemy</note> nor the integrity of the Fraternity <note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood, a secret society of doctors and sages</note>, nevertheless lose heart when they realize they have not been deemed worthy of such a great fellowship. Consequently, they invent reasons for slanders which they pour out against it; this chapter responds to them.*

From those who oppose the Fraternity directly, we now move toward those who oppose it indirectly. For these individuals do not deny the existence of the aforementioned brotherhood; however, they strive to dishonor it through either words or deeds, so that they might gain some personal honor or profit from doing so. Those who attack it with words believe they have sufficient cause to belittle or rebuke the members <note>original: "coarguendi"; to prove wrong or silence in argument</note>, by whatever means or occasion, for the reason that they themselves

<meta>catchword: themselves</meta>

<summary>Chapter 17 begins by addressing critics who support the principles of alchemy and the Rosicrucians but turn to slander out of spite after being denied entry into the order. The author identifies their motivations as a mix of wounded pride and a desire for personal profit.</summary>

...are neglected and not immediately enrolled in such a great council. For since they assume they have grasped something in <term>Alchemy<note>original: "Chymicis"; the study of transmuting matter and seeking the Philosopher's Stone</note></term> and other <term>cabalistic<note>original: "cabalisticis"; referring to esoteric mystical traditions</note></term> or secret matters (though we see some people putting such things forward that seem more like trifles than matters of great weight), they appear to themselves to be sufficiently experienced. In their own judgment, they believe they should be rightly recognized by the <term>Fraternity<note>The Rosicrucian Brotherhood</note></term> and preferred over others who either understand less or are further from the secret profession.

But the members of the order cautiously pretend not to notice these harsher characters; they are far from admitting them or taking them into their society. "Hence these tears" <note>original: "Hinc illæ lachrymæ"; a classical Latin idiom meaning "so that is the real source of the problem"</note> and the grief buried in their hearts. Indeed, if reason is not entirely blind, a certain injury is done to these "good men" by this neglect. They feel they ought to be placed among the <term>Triarii<note>original: "triarios"; the elite veteran soldiers of the Roman legion who formed the third and strongest line of defense</note></term> as proven veterans, yet they are not even ranked among the recruits. And since they believe they have cleansed the <term>Augean stable<note>original: "Augiæ ftabulum"; a reference to the Greek myth of Hercules cleansing a massive, filthy stable in a single day, used here as a metaphor for achieving a monumental reform or purification of knowledge</note></term>, why should they not be honored with a worthy reward?

But that society can be excused, because they require anyone to be associated with them—even those most worthy in terms of learning—to first be tested by a five-year silence (just as <term>Pythagoras<note>The Greek philosopher who required his students to maintain absolute silence for five years to cultivate self-control</note></term> did with his disciples), or even longer. This is done so that they might [restrain] their emotions with power and a bridle [of the tongue]...

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the requirements for admission into the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. ...should first learn to tame his tongue before he becomes a participant in such great secrets. Therefore, let these same individuals not lose heart, but let them wait for the outcome of events; perhaps something will come which is not currently hoped for. Let them seek after the matter with restraint, refraining from slanders and from their own habitual passion with which they burn deep within. If they should later find themselves frustrated in their hope, they should attribute the cause to their own rashness rather than to the unfair will of those others [the Fraternity]. For "**many are the wand-bearers, but few are the true followers of Bacchus.**" <note>original: "Multi enim Tyrſigeri, ſed pauci Bacchi"; a classical Greek proverb meaning many people take up the outward symbols of a mystery or wisdom, but few are truly initiated or divinely inspired.</note> Men who are not unworthy, as they judge themselves, are admitted into that society, but others are admitted who are far more worthy than they. 

"**Everyone has the spirit of a king within himself**," says <term>Seneca<note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher who wrote extensively on virtue and self-control.</note></term>, "**so that he would rather rule others than be ruled by them**"; yet no one inquires whether he truly possesses the <term>virtue<note>In this context, moral excellence and strength of character required for leadership or high office.</note></term> and disposition worthy of a king. Each man turns a blind eye to his own vices and defects; but regarding his own talents and praise, he maintains a "**serpentine eye**"—that is, one that is watchful and wide open. <note>In ancient lore, serpents were believed to be exceptionally vigilant because they appear to never close their eyes.</note> No one can say he is being neglected except where the <term>law<note>original: "ius"</note></term> and <term>custom<note>original: "mos"</note></term> designate him [as an heir], as in entering into inheritances, whether by a will or by <term>intestacy<note>The legal state of inheriting when someone dies without leaving a valid will.</note></term>, and other such matters. For this reason, only a fool would complain that he was not elected King of Persia or the Great <term>Mughal of India<note>original: "magnum Mogollum Indicum"; referring to the powerful rulers of the Mughal Empire, used here as an example of an office one cannot simply claim by right of desire.</note></term>. In the same way, those must be judged who do not [see]
