--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 116 regarding the qualifications for entering the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. It shifts from the necessity of controlling one's speech to the nature of true chastity and moral integrity, using examples from classical antiquity to illustrate that outward behavior or physical inability to sin is not the same as voluntary virtue.</meta>

...having been made participants of this society, they are vexed. Let them wonder why they are not answered according to their desires, even though they lead a celibate or blameless life. For this [outward lifestyle] is entirely accidental; it is neither strictly required by the Brothers, nor truly mastered by these applicants. To the aforementioned Colleges—the **Eleusinian** <note>The Eleusinian Mysteries were the most famous secret religious rites of ancient Greece, dedicated to Demeter and Persephone; they promised initiates rewards in the afterlife.</note> and others—only those of proven life and chastity were admitted. From these, even the **Emperor Nero** suffered a rejection; and **Antoninus** <note>Likely referring to the Roman Emperor Antoninus Pius or Marcus Aurelius, both noted for their stoicism and moral character.</note> desired to be initiated so that he might testify to the world the integrity of his life. 

Indeed, the **Hierophants** <note>original: *Hierophantæ*; the high priests who presided over the sacred mysteries and "showed the holy things" to initiates.</note> of these colleges were so intent on preserving their chastity that they rendered themselves impotent and "cold" by drinking the juice of hemlock, just as the priests of the goddess **Cybele** <note>An ancient Mother Goddess whose priests, the Galli, famously castrated themselves as an act of devotion.</note> castrated themselves entirely. Some in our own time have done the same—those who **act the part of a Curius** <note>original: *Curios simulant*; a reference to Manius Curius Dentatus, a Roman hero who was a symbol of ancient austerity. To "pretend to be a Curius" means to play the part of a stern moralist.</note> while **living for the Bacchanalia** <note>The wild, drunken, and unrestrained festivals of Bacchus.</note>. They do this not so much out of a love for chastity, as out of fear of the punishment that would befall the common people. 

But the virginity found in the crippled and the mutilated is not voluntary, but forced; the same is true for those who are naturally "cold" and impotent. That chastity which is truly voluntary, however, **burns** and inflicts painful emotions upon both the soul and the body, which leads to that cry of **Medea** <note>A reference to Ovid’s *Metamorphoses*, where the sorceress Medea struggles with her sudden passion for Jason: "Why, O royal maiden, do you burn for a stranger?" (original: *Quid in hospite regia virgo*).</note>: **"Why, O royal maiden, for a stranger?"**

Not every man is ready for every thing at all hours. Just as time is changed in every "now," so it subjects all **human affairs** to **change**, so that they differ from how they were before. I do not know whether one should more blame or praise **Hippocrates of Cos**, that famous physician, who—having been invited by **Artaxerxes**, King of the Persians, by a most kind letter full of many grand promises to come and treat his son—responded most unkindly that he would not come to a barbarian, but would practice medicine only for the Greeks. It is therefore more prudent for them <note>The Rosicrucian Brothers.</note> that, while writing, they should waste a little paper rather than lose their secrets, or things even dearer than those; or that others should strain their voices against their deaf ears, rather than that they should lose their own authority <note>In this context, *authoritas* implies their moral standing, personal safety, and the integrity of their secret knowledge.</note> through excessive compliance—and what is more important than that? Meanwhile, there is no doubt that they will not fail good men of the highest reputation at a convenient place and time in their necessities, if it can be done without prejudice or harm to themselves.

<meta>Continuing from page 127. The catchword "omni-" suggests the next page begins with a word like "omnibus" or "omnimodo".</meta>

...they disturb the springs, according to the saying, *And boars let into clear springs* <note>original: "Et liquidis immissi fontibus apros" — a quote from Virgil’s Eclogues (2.59), used here to illustrate how something pure is defiled by something coarse.</note>; these things serve to contaminate and infect the smoothness and splendor of bodies. In rich soil, many useless things grow up besides the sown vegetables, which suppress the good fruit; in sandy and leaner soil, this is not so. What should be done here? There is something even in the flowers themselves that causes pain; hence, <term>Heliogabalus</term> <note>A Roman Emperor (r. 218–222 AD) notorious in historical accounts for his decadence; he famously smothered guests with a literal "rain" of flower petals.</note> is said to have shut up his male and female servants in bedrooms filled with flowers, and tortured them with the very pleasure and scent of the blossoms. As <term>Apuleius</term> <note>A Roman philosopher and author of the 2nd century AD.</note> rightly said: *Where there is honey, there is gall; where there is plenty, there is a swelling.* <note>original: "Vbi mel, ibi fel, vbi vber, ibi tuber" — a rhyming Latin proverb about the proximity of pain to pleasure.</note> Small birds know how to separate the grain from the husks or the awns. Shall rational men alone, then, neglect to discern good from evil? But woe to you, worst of deceivers, who, while you shun working with your hands and seeking an honest livelihood, turn your minds to such deceits and tricks. The vengeance of the **Deity** <note>original: "Numinis"</note> awaits you, which compensates for its slowness with the gravity of the punishment. Or did you think this matter most suitable for cloaking your crimes? You are <term>swindlers</term> <note>original: "Stelliones"; literally "spotted lizards," but in Roman law, "stellionatus" referred to the crime of fraud or dishonest dealing that didn't fit other specific categories.</note> and are not immune from the crime of swindling. Depart, you <term>drones</term> <note>original: "fuci"; the male bees that do not collect honey; a common metaphor for lazy parasites living off the work of others.</note>, from this beehive, for you offer nothing but harm to our bees! Go, you **three-letter fellows** <note>original: "trium literarum socii"; a Latin idiom referring to the word "FUR," which means "thief."</note>, to the Pelasgian <note>A poetic term for Greek, often used to refer to distant or ancient lands.</note> business; you know what I mean. This harvest is not for you, for whom **hempen** <note>original: "Canabina"; referring to the hemp rope used for hanging.</note> things are more suitable than **golden** ones, such as are said to have been at <term>Crotone</term> <note>A city in southern Italy where Pythagoras established his famous school.</note> while **Pythagoras** was teaching there. You are not invited to this Pythagorean assembly, but go far from here, far away, you profane ones! It is unjust...

<summary>The author contrasts the natural instinct of animals to seek healing with the behavior of fraudulent "drones" or swindlers. He harshly condemns these deceivers, banishing them from his intellectual circle and suggesting they deserve the gallows rather than the "golden" wisdom of Pythagoras.</summary>

We know that sailors at sea do not always use full sails, but against crosswinds and side-winds, they set an oblique course until they reach the place they intended. The Indian Elephant <term>Elephas Indicus</term> <note>The elephant was a common symbol of strength, piety, and an indifference to small annoyances like stinging insects.</note> does not pause for a gnat, nor does the Parrot <term>Psittacus</term> heed the croaking of the "puff-cheeked" frogs <note>original: *physignatorum*; a reference to the character Physignathus from the ancient mock-epic *The Battle of Frogs and Mice*, used here to represent blustering, empty-headed critics.</note>. Let the frogs fall silent at the lit lamp, and let the Pan-gods <note>referring to Pan, the rustic god of the wild and unrefined sound</note> be quiet where Apollo <note>the god of light, music, and reason</note> sings.

Now, you brothers, wherever you may be dwelling, I call upon you and mindfully warn you: never allow the work you have performed yourselves until now, or what you intend to perform in the future, to be made empty of its effect. Instead, always be true to yourselves; that is, just as you excel all others in learning <term>doctrina</term> and wisdom <term>sapientia</term>, so should you excel them in humanity <term>humanitas</term> <note>In this context, *humanitas* refers to the Renaissance ideal of "the humanities"—the development of high moral character alongside intellectual study.</note>. # ANACREONTIC SONG,
## IN TWO PARTS,
### *A Panegyric to the Venerable Society of the <term>R.C.</term>* <note>The "R.C." refers to the **Rosicrucians** (Fraternitas Rosae Crucis), a secret society of doctors, alchemists, and philosophers that caused a sensation in 17th-century Europe by claiming to possess hidden wisdom that could heal the world.</note>

*May it be lawful for me to tell, throughout the lands of Germany,*
*of the great gifts of the hidden Brotherhood, blessed by the*
*<term>Hermetic</term> art; O you, be silent and favor me with your attention;* <note>original: *Hermeticâ beatæ*; referring to the "Hermetic" tradition of alchemy and magic attributed to the legendary figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>
*you are not among the many who are ignorant of hidden things.*
*You, the flower and glory of the German people, show yourself*
*kindly, and be present for these melodic meters;*
*know that I will give nothing merely for the pleasure of the ears,*
*but wish, according to custom, to offer words and deeds to the truth.*
*Mother Nature <term>Mother Nature</term> <note>original: *Natura mater*; personified here as the active agent of God's creation on earth.</note> governs the entire universe,*
*just as the Creator commanded her, and the nourishing goddess*
*observes certain laws, by the course of which*
*she produces all things, and bestows them with deep power and force.*
*If anyone penetrates her, either with sharper thought*
*or more perceptive use of the mind,*
*he becomes a participant in her more secret inner chamber:*
*Nor is there anything hidden that will be concealed from him,*
*which lies buried in the great breast of the Mother, who reveals herself to him.*
*Human life labors with such great business,*

Are cured without taking a fee or reward,
And this gift of health is offered freely to the sick,
No greater <term>prize</term> than health can be given;
When that <term>Pyrrhus</term>, King of <term>Epirus</term>, knew this, <note>Pyrrhus (c. 319–272 BC) was a powerful Greek king and general.</note> 
He sought only this gift from the gods in sacred places;
For if he held this, he thought he would attain
Whatever could be gained through labor or through skill in practice:
Shall I not call you brothers three and four times blessed?
Of your own accord you visit the sick through wide kingdoms,
And search through the world for those upon whom your light may shine,
Those abandoned by all and destitute of help and wealth,
Nor do you dedicate any rest to these endeavors.

<summary>This page uses classical mythology—specifically the quest for the Golden Fleece—to allegorize the Rosicrucian mission of bringing divine medicine from the East to Germany. It emphasizes the brothers' commitment to healing the poor for free, identifying health as the greatest possible prize.</summary>

<keywords>Jason and the Argonauts, Golden Fleece, Alchemical Medicine, Charity, German Wisdom, Chiron, Medea, Pyrrhus of Epirus, Healing the Poor</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Anacreontic Song" in honor of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. The previous page established the brothers as heirs to ancient wisdom and alchemical mastery. This page focuses on their specific scientific achievements, their mechanical inventions, and the harmony between their inner faith and outward works.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

Who could speak of your **Works** <note>original: *Erga*; a Greek term often used to describe primary labors or achievements.</note>, which must be 
More weighty to you? You possess the true
**Nuclei** of the sciences and the **marrow** of the arts;
You have noted all the herbs of the earth and the stars of the heavens,
And what hidden power resides within them:
Those twin springs flow for you eternally,
By which you are able to provide a thousand secrets of things:
Shall I not call you, O Brothers, thrice and four times blessed?
Such a great heat of sacred doctrine burns within you,
That you often read the divine laws of the Book <note>Referring to the Bible, emphasizing that their scientific study is secondary to their religious devotion.</note>,
It is a pleasing care for you to handle the gifts of the Gods:
Of **Vesta** and **Pallas**, of **Prometheus** and of the **Lemnian** <note>These represent the elements of their work: Vesta (the hearth/fire), Pallas Athena (wisdom and craft), Prometheus (the bringer of fire/technology), and the Lemnian god Vulcan (blacksmithing and metallurgy).</note>,
Fires and hearths with a wondrous art, which Nature gives:
Lest sacred **Healing** <term>Medela</term> <note>A term for restorative medicine or the alchemical elixir.</note> be lacking for the sick, nor for the metals.
Shall I not call you, O Brothers, thrice and four times blessed?
But those things just mentioned are called **Side-works** <note>original: *Parerga*; Greek for secondary tasks or hobbies, suggesting that even their great scientific feats are secondary to their spiritual mission.</note>,
Since you are so perfectly skilled <note>original: *ad unguem*; literally "to the fingernail," a Roman expression for finishing a sculpture so perfectly that a fingernail cannot feel a seam.</note>—as Fame reports of you—
That you cite all opinions in your own words:
This first care fatigues you, by night and by day;
Nor does it matter only to know such things, but you strive 
To follow the commands themselves in practice, so that Mind and Hand
May join in a treaty, and Heart and Mouth in a commerce of deeds:
Shall I not call you, O Brothers, thrice and four times blessed?
You hold more subtle things in all the arts,
Both the forms of **Automata** <note>Self-moving mechanical devices, which were seen as wonders of engineering and "natural magic" in the 17th century.</note> and fashioned orbs
Of the heavens are governed by their own motions, which **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and strategic skill.</note>
Showed, so that they might be made by the use of the craftsman's art:
Who could tell of the other structures of **Machines**
And the inventions of high genius, which are said to be
Hidden in your sanctuaries, so that the world might one day
Receive benefit from here, and sing praise to God.

<vocab>Brothers, science, art, Pallas (Athena), Prometheus, Nature, Healing, machine, Minerva</vocab>

<summary>This page praises the Rosicrucian Brothers for their mastery over botany, astronomy, and metallurgy, while emphasizing that their scientific "works" are secondary to their spiritual devotion and mechanical inventions. It highlights their ability to combine theoretical knowledge with practical craftsmanship, particularly in the creation of complex automata and astronomical models.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucianism, alchemy, metallurgy, automata, sacred science, botany, astronomy, union of theory and practice</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Anacreontic Song" in honor of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood. Building on the previous page's distinction between their scientific "side-works" and their true spiritual mission, this section details the specific rules that define their order and their commitment to blending into society while performing acts of mercy.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

*Shall I not call you, O brothers, thrice and four times blessed?*
*You do not deny that six laws <note>Referring to the six fundamental rules of the Rosicrucian order established in their founding manifesto, the *Fama Fraternitatis*.</note> bind you and arrange you*
*into a proper order, established by the Author <note>original: Authore; likely referring to the legendary founder, Christian Rosenkreuz.</note>,*
*To observe what they command results in nothing for your fellowship <term>sodalitas</term> <note>A term for a specialized brotherhood or society.</note>*
*among mortals but what is good and profitable;*
*You are bound by no specific profession, except to faithfully*
*heal <note>original: mederi; the first and most important rule of the Brotherhood was to heal the sick for free.</note> the sick throughout the Christian world:*
*From this, no specific clothing is prescribed, nor a monk's cowl <term>cucullus</term> <note>A pointed hood or garment associated with monastic orders; the Rosicrucians emphasized that they wore the common dress of the countries where they lived.</note>,*
*Nor is one single seat of residence to be forced upon you all.*
*Shall I not call you, O brothers, thrice and four times blessed?*

->THE END.<-

<vocab>Brothers, Fellowship, To heal, Christian, Cowl/Hood, Residence</vocab>

<summary>This concluding page of the poem outlines the core lifestyle of the Rosicrucian brothers, emphasizing their six rules, their lack of religious habits, and their primary mission of healing the sick. It highlights their philosophy of serving humanity while remaining inconspicuous within society.</summary>

<keywords>Rosicrucian rules, Fama Fraternitatis, healing the sick, monasticism, Christian Rosenkreuz, sodality, religious freedom</keywords>
